- **Update** [@namzu/openrouter](../../packages/providers/openrouter/README.md) — the package README, which is the only page that describes this driver — now documents reasoning, in both directions. The driver had zero references to it: a model that returns thinking (`nvidia/nemotron-3.5-lightning:free` returns both `message.reasoning` and `reasoning_details`) produced blocks the kernel never saw, and `chatStream` opened by refusing `thinking` outright, which is why the CLI's reasoning pane stayed empty while five other drivers rendered it. The request side now maps the SDK's controls onto OpenRouter's unified `reasoning` object (`enabled` + `budgetTokens` → `{enabled: true, max_tokens}`, `adaptive` → `{enabled: true}`, `disabled` → `{enabled: false}`, `effort` → `{effort}`), refusing `max` and `ultra` rather than sending a level this wire cannot carry; each shape was verified accepted by the live endpoint before it was written down. The response side emits reasoning fragments on the existing channel, with the index the wire supplies and a `done` marker when visible output or a tool call starts — an EMPTY `content` is not that boundary, and every reasoning frame on this wire carries one, so treating it as one would close each block on the frame that opened it. Two wire facts the page states because a reader would otherwise re-derive them wrong: the fragment arrives TWICE (a flat `reasoning` string and the same text in the indexed `reasoning_details`), so the array wins and the text is emitted once; and `completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens` reaches `usage.reasoningTokens`, a SUBSET of `completionTokens` rather than an addition, because thinking is already billed as output. Bump `minor`: two additive capabilities and one removed refusal, no default changed.
- **Creation** [Model prices, and what an absent one means](sdk/model-prices.md), because `ModelInfo.inputPrice` and `ModelInfo.outputPrice` became optional and no page described them. The page states the rule the type now enforces — absent means the driver does not know, `0` means free — names what each of the ten drivers publishes, and says how a reader should render an absence (`unknown`, never `$0.00`). This is the third page for a `ModelInfo` field group, after [Model-owned reasoning capabilities](sdk/model-reasoning.md) and the `contextWindow` change it follows, and it is the same argument: `9d6c482c` established that a placeholder is a measurement by the time it reaches a consumer, and the field where the measurement is a bill makes it worse rather than different. The distinction was already documented one level down, in `packages/sdk/src/pricing/index.ts` and its rate card, which separate `undefined` from a known zero for cost estimation; the page records that cost estimation was never on this path, so a run's total is unaffected by what a menu displays.
- **Update** [Slash commands](cli/slash-commands.md): the picker section now says what `(free)` means and, more usefully, what its absence means. A row is marked only when the provider reported both rates as zero, so a provider that published no rate gets no marker rather than a zero marker — the conclusion that made four provider menus read as entirely free. The section also records that under 70 columns the row's notes are rebuilt from a fixed vocabulary, so `(free)` had to be added there or the marker would have been silently dropped on the narrowest screens.

- **Update** The model picker marks a zero-priced model `(free)` and its search finds the word, which [Slash commands](cli/slash-commands.md) now states in its picker section: what search matches, what the marker means, and that neither sorts nor hides a row. The owner's goal was to use OpenRouter's free models — 25 of the 445 rows its public `/models` endpoint serves are zero-priced, and the picker already listed them live with no way to tell them apart. The data was being dropped one type wide: `ModelListing`'s `ok` arm was `Pick<ModelInfo, 'id' | 'name' | 'inputModalities'>`, and `ModelInfo.inputPrice`/`outputPrice` are per-million-token numbers the driver had already parsed. The pick now carries them as OPTIONAL fields rather than `Pick`ed outright, and that is the load-bearing decision: `ModelInfo` types both as required, so a driver with no price to give writes *something*, and a `Pick` that promises a price promises one the picker would have to trust. `describeProviderModels` is the only place a listing is produced, and it carries a value only when `Number.isFinite` accepts it, so a non-finite price arrives absent; `modelStep`'s `isFree` marks a row only when both prices are exactly `0`, tested with `=== 0` and deliberately not falsily — `undefined` and `NaN` are what a driver says when it does not know, and neither is evidence that a model costs nothing. A free prompt with a paid completion is not free, and is not marked. `Picker.tsx` rebuilds notes from keywords under 70 columns and dropped any word it did not know, so `(free)` joins `(default)` and `(image)` there instead of vanishing on a narrow terminal. Nothing is reordered, filtered or configured away: the provider's order stays the screen's order and the marker only adds words to rows that were already there. The bump is `minor` — the picker's search is a documented surface and it matches more text than it did, so a query that returned nothing can return rows, while no exported symbol, flag, config key or wire shape changes. The fixture rows are real: `google/lyria-3-pro-preview` (free, and the word appears in neither its ID nor its name), `nex-agi/nex-n2.5-mini:free` and `unbiased/pareto`, fetched from the same unauthenticated endpoint on 2026-09-18. **One finding is recorded and not fixed**, because it is a different change with a different bump: `anthropic`'s live path, `openai`, `codex` and `deepseek` write `0` into both price fields for every model they list — none of which is free — so those four lists would be marked free wholesale, and `google`'s `PRICES[id] ?? 0` and `openrouter`'s `m.pricing?.prompt ?? '0'` do the same for a model their own table or payload does not carry. That is the defect `9d6c482c` fixed for `contextWindow` by making the field optional and having the drivers omit it; the price pair is still required, and making it optional is an SDK change with a `major` bump, taken deliberately not here. `lmstudio` and `ollama` write the same zero and are the harmless case — a local model is not billed per token — so the marker is right for them by accident rather than by evidence.
- **Update** The Zen catalogue, refreshed from its sources, and one thing the refresh taught that the earlier entry above could not yet say. `union-alpha`'s absence was temporary: models.dev deleted its entry, the id was recorded as an omission with a reason and a note that it returns when the entry does, and hours later the service stopped serving it and both pages stopped routing it — so the **omission itself expired**, because the stale rule drops an omission once upstream neither documents nor serves the id. `packages/providers/zen/src/models.review.json` no longer names either spelling, the roster is unchanged at 59 Zen and 27 Go (the id was never carried, so no caller's routing changes), and [Zen](sdk/zen.md) now tells that two-step story where it used to describe the id as one the services still serve, with the escape hatch kept as the thing it was while the absence lasted. The same refresh reprices `gpt-5.6-sol` — input `2.0` → `4.0`, output `10.0` → `20.0` — because the page's 50% discount ran through 2026-09-18; the bump is `minor`, the convention this catalogue's own changeset states for additions and repricing.
- **Update** The sandbox entrypoint harness, no page edited and none created: nothing under `docs/` describes `entrypoint.test.ts`, and the image it tests is documented by its own file, which this does not touch. `entrypoint.test.ts`'s "returns rather than hanging when the init does not go" had been intermittently red on `main` — twice in a release run — with `expected 6 to be greater than or equal to 900`. The race was in the harness and not in the image: `entrypoint.sh` reads `/proc/<pid>/comm` before signalling and exits 0 the moment the name is not the init it expects, which is the fail-closed refusal the image must keep; `spawnStandIn` echoed `$!` for a child that had not `exec`'d yet, so inside that window the hook read `sh` where the symlinked `tini` was meant, refused, and skipped its whole wait. The case then measured the refusal instead of the flush, against a constant that looks nothing like the number it got. The fix is a bounded, loud-failing readiness poll routed through `spawnStandIn` so every stand-in call site gets it, waiting for the SPECIFIC name because that is what the hook turns on and one call site deliberately wants a plain `sleep` to stay one. It also refuses up front an expected name longer than the kernel can report — `/proc/<pid>/comm` keeps at most 15 characters, measured rather than assumed — so a name that could never match fails in a line instead of on a deadline. `entrypoint.sh` is deliberately untouched.

- **Update** [Cancellation and timeouts](sdk/cancellation-and-timeouts.md): three citations into `runtime/query/index.ts` re-pointed onto the lines they finally landed on. The #504 extraction had already re-derived them against the extraction's own tree; the park fixes then insert 22 lines above `boundedAdvisors` and above `IterationOrchestrator`, and a further 75 above the sandbox-acquisition `timeoutMs` — 64 from the first park round and 11 more from the review round that made `settleAbandonedRun` read the durable park state before it writes a verdict and set `settled` at both early returns. So `1273-1282`, `1340` and `1978` become `1295-1304`, `1362` and `2053`. Each span is byte-identical to what the page was pointing at, so no claim changed — only the line a reader is sent to. A sweep of the bundle finds no other `path:LINE` citation into a file the park fixes touched, and every other citation on the page still resolves.

- **Update** [Cancellation and timeouts](sdk/cancellation-and-timeouts.md): one citation re-pointed, because the #504 extraction moved the step-shaping members out of `runtime/query/iteration/index.ts` and every line below them shifted. The second `GuardCoordinator.beforeIteration` call site is now `:2240`, not `:2435` — the one the page names among "the only two call sites that enforce `timeoutMs`", with the first (`:375`) and the model call's own abort signal (`:788`) both ABOVE the moved region and therefore unmoved. Nothing else on the page cites the file below that point: the sweep over every `path:LINE` reference finds the other six into the files this refactor has touched still resolving to what they claim, and none past EOF. No claim changed — only the line a reader is sent to.

- **Update** [Cancellation and timeouts](sdk/cancellation-and-timeouts.md): every `path:LINE` citation into `runtime/query/index.ts` and `runtime/query/iteration/index.ts` re-derived against the #504 extraction that moved that code out of both files, which is what this page's opening promise ("every claim below was checked against this branch's source at the line cited") is owed when the file under it moves. Eleven citations were stale and are re-pointed: `withRecovery` to `prepare-run.ts:370-383` (from `index.ts:1174-1187`), `resilientProvider` to `prepare-run.ts:394-410` (`:1198-1214`), the composition-ordering comment to `prepare-run.ts:344-363` (`:1148-1167`) with the quoted sentence at `prepare-run.ts:355-358` (`:1159-1162`, whose opening `The` still sits at the END of the line rather than at the start of the next), `resolveProviderContextWindow` to `prepare-run.ts:191-223` (`:918-950`), the `RunContext` build's `provider: resilientProvider` to `prepare-run.ts:565` (`index.ts:1369`), `new IterationOrchestrator` to `index.ts:1340` (`:2046`), `boundedAdvisors` to `index.ts:1273-1282` (`:1979-1988`), the single `remainingUntilTimeoutMs()` caller to `index.ts:1978` (`:2684`), the model call's own abort signal to `iteration/index.ts:788` (`:887`), and the two `GuardCoordinator.beforeIteration` call sites to `iteration/index.ts:375` and `:2435` (`:474`, `:2786`). The citations into the two new modules are re-pointed and NOT rewritten: every line of the moved code is byte-identical, so each range is the same range one file over. Two citations gain a name rather than a number alone — the model call's signal now says which call it is on (`streamProviderTurn`, because that file holds seven byte-identical `signal: this.ctx.abortController.signal,` lines and the number alone no longer picked one out) and the advisory one names `boundedAdvisors` — because the line number is the fragile half of a citation, and the page is read by grepping for it. Line numbers are KEPT rather than replaced by symbol names: the page's contract is checking a claim at a cited line, and a symbol alone would not say where in the file the claim is. Scope of the re-derivation: all 57 citations on the page resolve and none is past EOF, and only the two files the refactor touched could have shifted at all — every other file the page cites is byte-identical to the commit its citations were verified against, so their lines are where they were. Nothing in the page's findings changed: only the line a reader is sent to. `generated.at` moves to today; the page stays `draft`.
- **Update** Query orchestration again: two more park defects fixed on the same day as the four above, and again no page edited and none created. The bundle still describes none of this surface — `findPendingCheckpoint`, `CheckpointManager`, `unpark`, `pruneKeepLast` and `hitlParkTtlMs` appear in `docs/` only in this log — so the record of what a host can observe is here and in the branch's changeset. (1) An answered `iteration_checkpoint` park is now resolved. A run that paused at the cadence, was resumed with `{action: 'continue'}` and then completed kept reporting the park as OUTSTANDING: `planPendingResume` applies a decision only to a `tool_review` or `user_question` park, because an `iteration_checkpoint` park leaves no unanswered tool call for one to reach, and the unpark ran only when there was a plan. Both consequences were reachable through public exports — `findPendingCheckpoint` kept serving a question nobody was waiting on, and a second `resumeRun` of the finished run was refused with `reason: 'awaiting-decision'` — and the F2 fix earlier the same day had made the row uncollectable as well, since `prune` skips an unresolved park. The decision IS carried out on that arm (continuing is what the loop does), so the park is resolved the way every other arm resolves one: the record stays, the request stays on it, only `pending` ends. A `pause` still holds a park rather than answering it. (2) A park whose batch crash recovery answered no longer records the human's decision as its answer. Recovery answers such a batch with explicitly unknown outcomes, and the park was nevertheless written down as decided by whatever the human had said — `{action: 'continue'}` for calls that never ran and were never approved. It is still resolved (leaving it outstanding would have `findPendingCheckpoint` serve it as the newest park, and a resume would then rewind the run to the crash's checkpoint and re-execute a batch it had moved past), but the record now carries `{action: 'pause'}` with a reason naming what ended it and which decision it superseded — the shape `expire` already uses for "this park ended and no decision was carried out", chosen there over `abort` for reading as a refusal. Both are `patch`: no export added or removed, no union widened, no default changed, and the only observable difference is whether a park is still served and what its `decision` field says. One assertion moved in `a-pause-on-the-cadence-parks-the-run.test.ts` and one in `a-park-survives-a-decision-that-does-not-apply.test.ts`, both written by the earlier round to pin the defective behaviour on purpose; each says so in its own comment.
- **Update** Query orchestration, four defects, no page edited and none created, because no page in the bundle describes any of the four internals: `pruneKeepLast`, `checkpointEvery`, `CheckpointManager.prune`, the plan gate's park, `ResultAssembler.finalize`'s position and `ToolExecutor.executeBatch`'s fill-the-holes loop appear in `docs/` only here. Three of the four changed behaviour and one changed only a claim. A Stop now resolves a run parked on plan approval: `runPlanGate` awaited `resumeHandler` directly where the tool-review and iteration-checkpoint parks race it against the run's abort signal, and the plan gate runs in the iteration loop rather than inside a tool call, so nothing downstream bounded the wait either; the park stays recorded before the await, so a host can still find and resume it. An abandoned run's durable record is now terminal: a host that breaks out of `for await` runs the run's `finally` and skips the `return await resultAssembler.finalize()` that sits after it, and since `finalize()` is the only caller of `RunPersistence.persist()`, the store kept what `init()` wrote — `idle`, which `deriveRunStatus` reads back as `queued` for a run whose jobs were killed and whose sandbox was destroyed. Such a run is recorded `cancelled`: no new `RunExecutionStatus`, no new `StopReason`, no new event, because nothing failed and there is no consumer left to emit to. `CheckpointManager.prune` no longer collects a checkpoint whose park is unresolved — it still keeps the newest `keepLast` and now skips unresolved candidates, so the row an approval queue serves is not deleted as growth control, and a host that never sets `pruneKeepLast` is unaffected since that gate still means "never prune". And `executeBatch`'s docblock no longer says every `tool_use` is answered "by construction": that holds for every path that returns and not for a call that throws, where `serial = serial.then(run)` skips every later serial call, `Promise.all` rejects, the fill-the-holes loop never runs and the batch produces no messages at all. The two behaviour changes are `patch`: no export, event shape, config key or default moved, and the only new `stopReason` value a host can observe is one that already existed. Two holes in that work were found by review and fixed at the tip: an abandoned run parked with an outstanding park is left parked rather than recorded `cancelled`, because `deriveRunStatus` reads a terminal status before it reads the park and `handleHITLDecision` drains `run_paused` BEFORE it sets its stop reason — so the in-memory state says nothing at the instant the park is already durable, and the durable state is what the guard now asks; and the resume path resolves a `plan_approval` park too, not only the cadence one, so a run resumed with `approve_plan` no longer completes with its park still outstanding and uncollectable. The same pass corrected the public `pruneKeepLast` docblock in `types/run/config.ts`, which still promised oldest-first deletion across all checkpoints including park ones, gave the superseded-park record a comparable marker (`PARK_SUPERSEDED_BY_RECOVERY`) instead of prose, and had the two early settles (sandbox-acquisition and input-guardrail returns) set the settled flag and persist through `finalize()` rather than leaving the write to the abandoned-consumer branch. No page describes any of it, so none was edited. The same branch also de-flaked one test with no behaviour change: `the-window-is-asked-once.test.ts`'s "falls back and runs when metadata stays pending" drove a run with `timeoutMs: 20`, which is simultaneously the run's budget and the context-window resolver's private deadline, and then asserted the run settled inside a 1000 ms poll — the two 20 ms deadlines raced on a real clock, so the case failed in both directions (no request, and a second request) in three of five full-suite runs while passing every time alone. It now runs under a fake clock advanced exactly once by exactly the deadline, reading the resolver's aborted signal before it waits on anything; the assertions are unchanged and none of them rest on a wall clock, which is why no page was edited for it either.

- **Creation** [Firecracker sandboxes](sdk/firecracker-sandbox.md), the page for the owned `microvm` tier — its provider config, the reserve-then-execute path, and the per-phase exec timing issue #468 asked for. It is a new page rather than an edit because the tier had none: `MicroVMBackendConfig` was described only by its own docblocks, and this change puts a new field on it. The public surface is `MicroVMBackendConfig.onExecTiming` (`packages/sandbox/src/index.ts`), forwarded by `pickBackend` into `FirecrackerBackendInternalConfig.transport` and so to `VsockTransportOptions.onExecTiming` on the transport (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/firecracker/transport.ts`), whose payload type `FirecrackerTransportTiming` is exported from the package entry point. It is named `onExecTiming` and not the Kubernetes tier's `onTiming` because `KubernetesTransportOptions` extends these options and already spends that name on a four-phase payload of its own; both sides of that inheritance now say so in place — a Kubernetes host reads it where it looks for its timing hook, and the new page repeats it for anyone reading across the two tiers. The bump is `minor`: three additive declarations, no default changed, nothing removed.

- **Update** The tier's exec path, so that an exec's wall clock can be attributed to a named phase instead of guessed at. The four phases are the ones the Kubernetes tier already reports — `dialMs`, `reserveMs`, `executeMs`, `drainMs` — measured at the same places and with the same meanings, because this transport is the one that tier wraps. Three more are this tier's own, measured inside `executeRaw` from the instant the execute request is written: `firstFrameMs` (request written → the guest first spoke), `terminatorMs` (request written → the zero-length terminator frame) and `peerCloseMs` (terminator → the peer's own close, and only a close the PEER caused — a `close` event that arrives after the guard has already destroyed the socket is this transport reacting to its own `socket.destroy()`, and crediting it would publish the guard's own constant as a duration the peer took, so that handler now returns on an already-settled call, the rule `readFileStream` applies in the same position). Those three are absent, not zero, when their phase was never reached; a dial that never connected contributes nothing to `dialMs`, exactly as it never fires `onDial`. The adapter and its `RemoteExecutionController` are now built per call rather than held on the transport, so the accumulator behind the hook is per-call state — the arrangement, and the reason for it, that `KubernetesAgentTransport.exec` already had. Over the suite's unix-socket loopback peer, `sh -c 'printf out-line'` reports `dial=2 reserve=3 execute=5 drain=0 first=4 term=4 close=0` ms: printed by that suite's own case, which asserts the relations between the numbers rather than these values, and quoted in the page as the local measurement it is.

- **Update** Issue #468's second ask, which is answered as a documented contract rather than a fix, because it is not reproducible in this repository. No constant was removed and no number was invented. `POST_RESPONSE_CLOSE_TIMEOUT_MS` (1 s, `firecracker/transport.ts`) now carries what it actually is: a **reject-only guard** that can only turn a socket whose peer never closes into `vsock transport: exec peer did not close after terminator` — every resolution path in that file requires the peer's `close` event, so raising it buys no slow success and lowering it fails a merely slow peer. What the page states in its place is the half-close contract a host-owned relay has to honour, together with the arithmetic that keeps it from being used as an explanation it cannot be: the guest calls `socket.end()` after the terminator, the host resolves on that close, and a relay that forwards payload but holds the FIN (its own idle timer, a full-duplex buffering policy, a proxy waiting for the guest process to exit) transfers that wait onto every exec — a hold UNDER a second is reported as `peerCloseMs`, and a hold AT OR PAST a second does not make an exec slow, it makes it FAIL, with the named rejection and no `peerCloseMs` at all. So a deployment whose execs resolve has already proved that window was under a second, and the ~1.05 s issue #468 reports cannot be hiding in it; the page says so rather than leaving the relay hypothesis standing beside the evidence that excludes it. The triage that preceded this change drove the production path over loopback and measured exec wall clock of 3–12 ms, which is why the ~1.05 s is attributed to a component outside this package and why the honest deliverable is the instrumentation plus the contract, not a deletion.

- **Creation** `docs/security-report.md`, the project security report assessed at `f58a086f`, landed from a PR opened 2026-08-13 and never merged. Its status section was rebuilt rather than carried over, because `main` has 745 commits the branch does not and the delta table it shipped had started to over-claim: NAMZU-SEC-004 was marked landed on the strength of an artifact that closes only its inward-address half, while the other half is tracked by issue #398, open, and a reader takes "Landed" as closed — the failure the report itself files as NAMZU-SEC-016. Every line of the table was re-checked against `main` at `c17993af` for the file it names and what that file does, not by re-testing a finding, and each row now carries `Landed` or `Partly`, the half that remains, and its issue where one exists. #398 (SEC-004), #402 (SEC-005) and #427 (SEC-008) are the open ones; SEC-001, SEC-002, SEC-003 and SEC-007 are partly discharged with no issue tracking the rest. Four findings whose status moved since the snapshot are rows the original table did not have (SEC-001, SEC-002, SEC-007 and SEC-014), SEC-006's row now names `packages/cli/src/config/load.ts` — the file that actually refuses an unreadable configuration — instead of `packages/cli/src/context/sandbox.ts`, which is where the CLI attaches its sandbox and belongs to SEC-007, and SEC-009, SEC-010, SEC-011 and SEC-015 are recorded as re-checked and unchanged. The findings themselves are untouched: a dated assessment edited to match later code stops being a record of what was true. Frontmatter was rebuilt in OKF shape (`type: Analysis`, `status: draft`, `generated`, matching the sibling audits) in place of the old `okf_version`/`uid`/`diataxis`/`owner`/`timestamp`/`lastReviewed`/`status: active` block, none of which any other page in the bundle uses; the closing Related list, whose nine links pointed at pages the OKF migration deleted, now points at pages that exist; and the report was added to `docs/index.md` under a new `# Pages` heading, since it is the bundle's first page at the root rather than in a directory.

- **Creation** [The container sandbox worker's credential](sdk/container-sandbox-worker.md), the page that had no counterpart: the container backend's worker (`packages/sandbox/worker/server.js`) served `/execute`, `/read-file` and `/write-file` with no authentication of any kind, and no page described that surface at all — the only sandbox page was the Kubernetes one, whose per-instance credential is the guest agent's, a different process on a different wire. The page records what the worker now requires (`Authorization: Bearer <NAMZU_SANDBOX_TOKEN>` on every route but `GET /healthz`, `401 {"error":"unauthorized"}` with nothing else in it, the gate running ahead of every dispatch so the route table is not enumerable and no handler or execution lease is reached), where the token comes from (minted by `spawnDockerSandbox` at `create()` time — 32 `randomBytes`, per instance, handed to the docker CLI in its OWN environment and resolved by a VALUELESS `--env NAMZU_SANDBOX_TOKEN` rendered last among the `--env` flags, so the value is in no argv `ps` would show and a host's own `options.env` entry under the same name cannot displace it; a non-zero `docker run` renders its argv with every `--env` value redacted, so the error a host logs carries the keys and never the secret), where it IS readable (the container's own config, so `docker inspect` shows it for the container's life to anyone who can already talk to the daemon, and the worker's `/proc` to a workload sharing its uid — both stated on the page rather than left to be discovered), how the client presents it (`HttpWorkerClient` on `reserve`/`cancel`/`execute` plus the backend's two direct `fetch`es for `read-file`/`write-file`, which are not client methods and had to be given the header separately), and the startup decision that makes the `0.0.0.0` default defensible: no token on a routable bind refuses to start, no token on loopback starts (nothing outside the netns can open that socket), `NAMZU_SANDBOX_TOKEN` set but empty OR padded refuses in every mode (a padded value can never be presented, so the worker would boot authenticated and refuse its own host), and `NAMZU_SANDBOX_ALLOW_UNAUTHENTICATED=1` is the named escape that gives the credential up rather than deferring it — matched untrimmed and case-insensitively, because a security escape that accepts `" yes "` is the same trap as one whose off-spelling turns it on. A failed `listen` now exits 1 with the address named instead of exiting 0 with an unhandled error, which is what made a port race read as a successful refusal. The ACI half of the issue is recorded as OUT OF SCOPE and not implemented, and the reason it was first recorded with was WRONG in the direction that mattered. The page had said a per-claim secret "has no channel into a claimed container group" because the claim API admits only config maps; the claim API's one admitted override IS a config map, and on Linux a config map is mounted into the container as a file under `/mnt/configmap/<containername>/<key>`, so a caller-supplied per-claim value does reach the claimed group. The channel exists. The credential is still declined there, for the two reasons that survive their source: the worker reads its token from `process.env` once at startup and never looks at the filesystem for one, so a mounted value is not read; and Microsoft's own guidance is that config-map values are not included in the security policy and are not validated by the runtime before the file mount is made available, with values that could affect application security to be given to the container as environment variables instead. That is the ANSWER to the issue's design question 1, recorded on the page with the concrete change that would close the gap — the backend sending a random per-claim value in the claim's config map and the worker reading it from the mount — which is deliberately NOT implemented in this round, because a credential path that cannot be exercised against a live standby pool from here would ship unverified. What an operator runs today is written down as the address first (the existing `subnetId` refusal, unchanged) and the escape second, since this backend's client is constructed without a token, its two direct `read-file`/`write-file` fetches send no header, and there is no field a caller could put one in. Four smaller corrections landed with it: the redaction helper compared each argv element to the literal `'--env'`, so four of the five spellings of that flag (`-e K=V`, `--env=K=V`, `-e=K=V`, the attached `-eK=V`) would have printed a credential into a failed `docker run`'s message — it now covers every spelling and each is pinned by its own test; the claim that an unauthenticated caller "cannot tell a retiring worker from a serving one" was false, since the poison check answers ahead of the `/healthz` dispatch and one bit of drain signal is readable without a token, which is stated as intended rather than left as a contradiction; a token carrying a code point above U+00FF (or a C0 control other than HTAB) booted a worker whose own client's `fetch` throws on it, and is now refused at startup like the empty and padded values, with the accepted set — HTAB, printable ASCII and U+0080-U+00FF — mapped by probing rather than assumed; and the sentence saying the credential rides "the same channel" as the workspace variables now says what is actually shared (the destination, not the argv, because those three are rendered with their values and this one is not). The page also states what is NOT covered: the transport is plain HTTP, so the token is replayable by anything on the path and is defence in depth behind network placement rather than a replacement for it, and a workload inside the container can still read the worker's own `/proc/<pid>/environ` — which is why the credential is per-instance. Verified by probing the real worker as a subprocess over loopback, not by reading it, and by a raw socket for the header shapes `fetch` will not produce — a duplicated `Authorization` (the FIRST one wins), an obs-folded header (the parser answers a bare `400` before the router runs), and the four request shapes that are not exactly `GET /healthz` (`POST /healthz`, `GET /healthz?x=1`, `GET /healthz/`, `HEAD`), each gated and each a `404` once the token is presented; the unpresentable-token boundary was mapped by sending every code point from U+0000 to U+0021 plus DEL, U+0080, U+00FF, U+0100 and U+20AC through `fetch` and reading back what arrived. The suites that hold it are named on the page; each of the five redaction spellings has its own case.
- **Update** The `@namzu/sandbox` README and the docblocks that described the old behaviour: the README's new section "The container worker's control API, and its token" carries the same facts for a reader of the package — the route table with and without the token, the minter, the argv-free channel it travels in and the two places it IS readable, the five-row startup table, the escape and what it gives up, and the warm-pool gap — beside the existing "Guest agent transports and the per-instance token" section, which describes the OTHER transport and had been the only place a reader could look for anything about sandbox credentials. The worker's own docblock no longer says "Authn: none"; that paragraph now says what the worker requires, why the bind default is still `0.0.0.0`, and repeats that the transport is not confidential. `src/backends/docker/index.ts`'s "Trust model" claimed the worker "only listens on loopback inside its own netns", which was false — the default bind is every interface, and it has to be, because a published container port forwards to the container's interface address rather than to its loopback — so the claim now names the credential as what makes the default safe. `worker/Dockerfile`'s "Trust model" said the worker "authenticates nobody" and now says what it requires, and its `ENV` block carries a note that no token is baked in there on purpose (one credential for every container ever built from the image, readable by anything that can pull it) and that a blank one is refused at startup. `aci-standby-pool/index.ts` and its public-address test are updated to point at the new page rather than at a docblock paragraph that no longer exists, and its `read-file`/`write-file` paths now carry the 401 hint the container backend's do — with wording specific to that backend, because a token is not something it can present and its operator needs to know which of the two situations they are in. `docs/sdk/index.md` lists the new page.

- **Creation** [Container-tier egress](sdk/sandbox-egress.md), a page for the boundary `container:docker` enforces an allowlist policy at — the tier's egress had no page, and the one it did have was in the negative: `kubernetes-sandbox.md` and `CHANGELOG.md` both described the container tier by contrast, as the backend that answers a host allowlist with an `HTTP_PROXY` environment variable. It now states the topology (the proxy as a sibling container, dual-homed between the sandbox's `--internal` network and an ordinary bridge, joined by `docker network connect --alias namzu-egress`), the exact reach a sandbox keeps afterwards argued from the flags, the three things a deployer must now supply, and the limits that are unchanged — domain fronting inside a `CONNECT` tunnel, a `resolver` policy now resolved at `create()`/`setNetworkPolicy()` rather than per request, `setNetworkPolicy()` replacing the proxy container rather than swapping a list in place, a brokered credential now readable by anything with daemon access rather than only by the creating process, and the proxy's listener being reachable by whatever else shares `egressProxyUpstreamNetwork` (default `docker`'s `bridge`). It also says what is NOT measured: no test in this repository starts a container, so the change is verified at the argv and plan level, in the same tier of evidence #378 reported for the container-hardening work.

- **Update** [Container-tier egress](sdk/sandbox-egress.md) and `packages/sandbox/README.md` again after review, on three claims that outran the code. (1) "One destination, and it is the boundary" was wrong about the sandbox's own internal network: it is a subnet, so a second sandbox on it — and that sandbox's proxy — is reachable too, which the page now states as what it is, with the remedy (one internal network per sandbox or per trust domain). (2) The brokered-credential disclosure named only "anything with access to the docker daemon" and missed the process table; the value now travels in the `docker` CLI child's ENVIRONMENT rather than its argv (an argv is world-readable in `/proc/<pid>/cmdline`, an environment only by its owning user), and the page says so — along with the pre-existing fact that `createSandboxProvider` has never forwarded `brokeredCredentials` at all. (3) Two refusals that exist are now documented: `egressProxyUpstreamNetwork` set to `'none'` or to the internal network itself, each of which leaves the proxy with no route out. The page's evidence section also gained the abort cases and the cross-boundary parse that were added to the tests.

- **Update** `packages/sandbox/README.md`'s container section and `packages/sandbox/src/index.ts`'s `ContainerBackendConfig`, both of which described `network` as the container's configuration and left the allowlist unmentioned: `network` now carries the requirement that a `static` or `resolver` policy needs that network to be `--internal` and `hostReachability` to be `'container-network'`, and two new config keys are documented there — `egressProxyImage` (required for an allowlist policy, refused without it) and `egressProxyUpstreamNetwork` (the proxy's route out, default `bridge`). The `EgressProxy` docblock gains `bindHost` and `selfNames`, the two options the container entrypoint sets and the host-side proxy does not.

- **Update** [Container-tier egress](sdk/sandbox-egress.md), `packages/sandbox/src/index.ts`'s `ContainerBackendConfig.network` and `packages/sandbox/egress-proxy/Dockerfile` after a verification review of `2b851cb9`, on two blockers and on claims that outran their evidence. (1) `setNetworkPolicy()` could leave a credential-bearing proxy container with a live route to the internet and nothing that would ever remove it: its lifecycle check ran before its first `await`, so a teardown landing while the swap was suspended inside `restartEgressProxyContainer` — a `destroy()`, or the `retire()` an in-flight `exec` triggers on a worker whose cancellation outcome is unknown, which is reachable with no host-side race at all — let the swap start a NEW `namzu-egress-<id>` after the teardown had removed everything else, and `egressProxyContainer` is only removed from `teardownSandbox` and `cleanupOnFailure`, both of which had already run. The swap now re-reads its lifecycle after the replacement's start returns, removes the replacement on a reconciler that takes no caller signal, and fails with the sandbox's own retirement instead of reporting a policy change on a sandbox that is gone; the removal sits after the container comes into existence rather than in a check before the `docker run`, which is what closes the ordering instead of narrowing it, and the page states both that and the limit it does not cover — a `destroy()` whose own `signal` was already aborted issues no `rm` at all, so the whole container set is left in that case, not just the proxy. (2) The sentence the last round claimed to have corrected was still shipping in two places: `src/index.ts`'s `network` field, which is the text an IDE shows a TypeScript consumer hovering the option and which also ships in the published `src`, and `egress-proxy/Dockerfile`. A grep for the claim found four more — the docker backend's file header and two refusal messages, `egress-proxy/server.mjs`'s header and its `fail` docblock, the changeset, and two test docblocks — and all of them now say what is true: there is no route off the internal network, the only way the sandbox's traffic reaches the internet is that container, and what else a host attaches to that network is reachable from the sandbox too. (3) The evidence claim was corrected rather than left inflated, and the probes were made to carry it: the fake daemon now keeps a registry of the containers it has committed, so "removed" is asserted as "gone" rather than as "an `rm` was typed" — the difference the swap race lives in, where the removal is issued before the container exists — four cases hold that daemon at a chosen point and abort there (the proxy's `docker run`, its attach, after it is up, and now the replacement's `docker run` during a live swap, torn down while it starts), each confirmed to fail against the shape it replaces, and the two swap failure paths are named as pins rather than probes, with the attach one stated as NOT mutation-distinguishable because the swap hands that start no signal. Also, from the same review: the entrypoint's refusal for an unparseable configuration no longer forwards V8's message, which embeds a snippet of the input — the whole policy, brokered credential values included — into the container's stderr; `parseProxyConfig` throws instead of calling `process.exit(1)` when the module is imported rather than run as the container's command, so a refusal can be asserted on without taking the vitest worker down; and two sentences that no longer matched the code were corrected — the docblock claiming the proxy's start ran before the cleanup it is now inside, and "the one way this file removes the proxy", which `teardownSandbox` has never been bound by.

- **Update** The Zen catalogue gate learned to refuse rather than to report, after review executed it against a fixture that reproduces the committed module exactly (so exit 0 was the agreement path, not an accident). Three findings, all closed, plus one corrected claim. **A route row the script stopped reading was dropped mid-sentence**: the row pattern is all-or-nothing per line with no counter, so a row whose id carried an underscore, one with a trailing space, and one with an empty package cell each vanished from the roster and left the run reporting `matches its source` — and CI is the only place this script runs. `parsePage` now refuses such a line (exit 2) and names it; the rule is two shapes, both measured against the live pages, where they flag nothing: two or more backticked cells, or three or more cells with at least one (corrected in the bullet below — at four, a row that lost its package column still passed). A price row no route row names is reported rather than ignored. **A reshaped models.dev was reported as a decision, and the roster had no floor**: renaming `limit` to `limits` across every entry produced `ZEN CATALOGUE NEEDS A DECISION` naming every model with the remedy "omit it in models.review.json" — and omitting every model then wrote `0 zen, 0 go` and reported green. Metadata that exists and has lost its fields is now a shape problem (exit 2), and a roster that comes out empty or falls past `ROSTER_FLOOR` (80% of what is committed) is refused as unusable rather than offered as a choice, with the floor stated in the message and the escape named. **Removals are no longer silent**: the script reads the committed module back, so a refresh prints `go REMOVED: omen-alpha` and a drift report names every added, removed and changed field instead of only the first line that differs — which is what `docs/sdk/zen.md` already promised. `protocolFor` also stopped being a substring test: `@ai-sdk/anthropic` on `https://evil.example/messages` used to be accepted, and now the host, the service prefix, the exact path and the package must all agree. `--from DIR` now replaces the module under test as well as the sources, so the agreement path and the exit-2 paths are exercised hermetically by 47 tests rather than only by the networked gate.

  Two corrections to this change's own first draft, both from that review. The changeset's `omen-alpha` justification was wrong: the Go service still SERVES it — `401 AuthError "Missing API key"`, the same answer `kimi-k3` gives, where an unknown id answers `401 ModelError` — so "will not work on the documented Go route either" contradicted both the evidence and its next clause. The model is still served and no longer documented, which is a different sentence and now the one written: the id is dropped from the roster because no wire can be derived for it, and a consumer can keep calling it with an explicit `protocol` (`client.ts` resolves `this.config.protocol ?? info?.protocol`), which the changeset now says. The bump intent is `major` rather than `minor`, because a consumer that pins the removed id gets a hard failure after the upgrade, and the changeset now states that future refreshes stay `minor` for additions and repricing and go `major` when an id is dropped. One review finding was NOT reproduced and is reported rather than "fixed": the free-model regression test is not defeated — with the section terminator removed the fixture yields two free names and the assertion fails — but only its `length` assertion was load-bearing, since the captured notes bullet is the whole sentence rather than the model name the `includes` check looked for. Both assertions are now load-bearing and the fixture states the adversary it carries.


- **Update** `@namzu/zen`'s model catalogue is generated rather than pinned, after the owner reported that the Zen models do not update by themselves and that new free ones keep arriving. `packages/providers/zen/src/models.ts` carried a `Snapshot: 2026-09-07` header, and its `listModels()` filtered the service's live `/models` answer through `findZenModel`, so the live endpoint could only ever SUBTRACT from a frozen list: a model the snapshot did not know was dropped in silence. Re-deriving the roster from the service's own pages found twelve Zen ids and eleven Go ids that the live endpoint advertises and the snapshot did not carry, including the two the owner's complaint is about — `union-alpha`, which the pages added as a free anonymous stealth model after the pin, and `deepseek-v4.1-flash` on Go. The fix is `scripts/generate-zen-models.mjs`: routes and base-tier prices come from `zen.mdx` and `go.mdx` on OpenCode's default branch, limits, tool support, modalities and effort options from `models.dev`, and `packages/providers/zen/src/models.review.json` holds the curation decisions upstream does not encode. A documented model must be either carried or omitted with a reason, and the CI gate **Zen catalogue matches its source** fails naming every model that is neither, so a new upstream model is now a decision somebody makes instead of a row that never arrives. The gate exits 1 for drift and 2 for a source that was unreachable or changed shape, and never skips.

  The derivation was checked before it was trusted: rendered from the pinned revision it was given, it reproduces all eighty-six committed entries byte for byte, and the only difference in the regenerated file was its header. The refresh adds `union-alpha` to Zen (anonymous, Messages wire) and `union-alpha` plus `deepseek-v4.1-flash` to Go, drops `omen-alpha` from Go because upstream's page no longer routes it, and corrects the two Go DeepSeek entries to the off-peak prices the page now states.

  Two findings are recorded rather than fixed, because neither is a catalogue defect. **The runtime cannot do this work.** The service's `/models` answer carries `id`, `object`, `created` and `owned_by` and no wire, so live discovery can confirm that a model exists and nothing about how to call it; and the one cross-service source that states a package, models.dev's own `npm`, is stated per PROVIDER — `@ai-sdk/openai-compatible` for both services, which would send the five Go Qwen route rows, documented on `/messages`, to Chat Completions — and per model for fewer than half the documented entries, so it cannot route the rest. A runtime that trusted either would send those to the wrong endpoint, so the roster is derived and reviewed instead of fetched per request. **Anonymous access is refused upstream.** Every free model, including `muse-spark-1.3-contributor-free` — which `docs/sdk/zen.md` records as verified working on 2026-09-08 — answered HTTP 403 `FreeTierError` on 2026-09-18, over `/responses` and `/messages`, with the public sentinel in the header that wire uses and with the driver's attribution headers attached. That is a change to the service's admission policy and is unaffected by this one; the page now carries the date and the evidence instead of the older promise that public models work without an account key.

- **Update** `packages/providers/zen/src/__tests__/anonymous.test.ts` no longer assumes the anonymous models share a wire. `union-alpha` is served over Messages, so the fixture learned the Anthropic event stream, the expected path is read from the roster, and the sentinel is asserted in whichever header the wire uses — `x-api-key` on Messages, a bearer on the other two. The test's old claim that everything except Muse is Chat Completions was false the moment a free model arrived on another route, which is exactly the staleness this change is about.

- **Update** The Zen catalogue gate reads a third source — what the service actually SERVES — and a scheduled job proposes the refresh, because the owner's complaint was that new free models do not arrive by themselves and a gate that only reads the pages cannot see a model no page documents. **The measured gap, closed in the direction it was open.** `GET /zen/v1/models` answers 71 ids and `GET /zen/go/v1/models` 38, both anonymously; twelve of them — three on Zen, nine on Go — were served while neither carried nor omitted, `deepseek-v4-flash-free` and `muse-spark-1.2-contributor-free` among them, and `listModels()` dropped every one in silence. `--check` now compares the roster against those two answers and reports each such id; it is exit 1 and not 2 because a wire is stated on a page and nowhere else, so the decision is a curation one, while an answer that cannot be READ is exit 2 like any other source — never a quiet pass. All twelve are now recorded in `src/models.review.json` with a per-id reason, none of them carried: every one is absent from its service's page, so no route for any of them could be derived from what the service serves. An omission for an id the service still answers is no longer stale, because the page is not the only source that says a model exists. `NAMZU_ZEN_MODELS_BASE` selects the host, the seam a test drives against a loopback server, and the fetch itself is only reached when a `--from` fixture does not carry the roster. **The scheduled refresh.** `.github/workflows/zen-catalogue-refresh.yml` re-derives the catalogue daily (04:17 UTC — the pages are edited by hand, so an hour's resolution buys nothing) and opens a pull request carrying the generator's own added/removed/changed report and the served-but-uncurried list; a source it cannot read fails the run loudly, and a run that changed nothing opens nothing. The commit carries the changeset the refresh needs, its bump read MECHANICALLY from that report — a removal is `major`, because a carried id stops resolving, and an addition or a repricing is `minor` — because nothing in this repository enforces a changeset on a pull request, so a refresh merged without one is a consumer-visible change that ships to nobody. The staging is exactly the two paths that matter, guarded: the generator's only write is `models.ts`, and a run that writes anything else fails the job rather than opening a pull request missing a file. It leaves `ci.yml`, `release.yml` and `check-workflow-gate-parity.mjs` untouched, which stays green — that check compares those two workflows, and a scheduled job is neither path onto `main`. **The dropped column.** Review's blocker was the same class as the last one: a route row that KEPT its values and LOST its package column is three cells with one code span, which the shape test read as prose, so `| Phi Newcomer | phi-newcomer | \`…/chat/completions\` |` left the roster with no message while the run printed `matches its source` — the invariant the gate is named for. The shape test now takes three or more cells, re-measured against both live pages where the wider rule flags zero lines; the backtick guard is what keeps it off the `| --- |` separator. **A renamed `tool_call` is a source, not a decision.** It was the one models.dev field whose loss fails QUIETLY: absent reads as "no tools", so the whole roster would have been rewritten to `supportsToolUse: false`, reported as drift with "run the generator" as the remedy, and gone green once somebody did. Every entry at once is now exit 2, as with `limit` and `modalities`; one entry of many is still a real answer. **A missing formatter is exit 2.** `biomeEntryPoint()` threw outside every catch, so a tree without `node_modules` printed a raw stack and exited 1 — the code that means "a person can decide this". **And both reports print.** A run with a pending decision used to exit before the added/removed/changed list, so drift was hidden behind it; the decision, the roster changes and the served list are printed together now, whatever the exit code. Three nouns are corrected with the same commit: models.dev's `npm` is stated per provider, and per model for a subset, rather than "per-model" throughout; Go has five Qwen route rows, not four; and the generated module header, `docs/sdk/zen.md` and the changeset say so.

- **Creation** [Tool-result screening](sdk/tool-result-screening.md), the page the whole boundary has been missing: `resultGuardrails` has been a public config key since it shipped and no page in the bundle described it, the context, the verdicts or either preset. The page states where a screen sits and why that position is structural (the registry returns to the executor, the executor applies the output budget and spills what is over it, compaction summarises later still), the five fields a screen is handed and the one that makes the context worth having (`provenance`, since a screen reading only the value cannot tell a connected server's words from a first-party tool's), the four verdicts and why `refuse` and `halt` are two words rather than one, and that a screen which throws fails closed. It documents both presets, and — at the same length as what the correspondence screen decides — **what it does not decide**: a lookup answering about the wrong subject needs a subject declaration the framework cannot infer, because a file read's answer is the file's contents and does not name the path, so "an answer mentions its subject" would refuse every ordinary read; an empty result for a non-empty request is real and is not a signal; a shape contradicting `ToolDefinition.outputSchema` would require carrying a schema the context does not have and enforcing a field documented as shown-to-the-model-never-validated; a failed call is left alone because a refusal replaces the output, trading an echo nobody needs caught for the diagnostic the model needs to read; and a result that is not a string is passed rather than refused, because a screen that throws fails closed and would turn its own bug into a refusal. **It records the scope, which is the correction that came out of review**: the screen judges results a CONNECTED server produced, because for this process's own tools the same observation is not a signal — `web_fetch` returns a page body, and a page whose body IS the URL it was fetched from is a true result from a working tool (`packages/sdk/src/tools/builtins/web.ts`, output assembled from `result.body`), so the first version refused it. `scope: 'all'` adds host tools and costs an exemption list; `passthroughTools` accepts the registered name, the plugin-qualified name, the bare name the server's own manifest uses, and `server:tool`. **And it records who chooses the screens, which is the other half of the review**: a run installs `DEFAULT_TOOL_RESULT_GUARDRAILS` (one correspondence screen) on the tool context of every run; a registry built with `resultGuardrails` wins, including `[]`; the run option `toolResultGuardrails` overrides the default where the registry declared none, on `runAgent`, `BaseAgentConfig`, `QueryParams` and `ToolContext`; and the CLI names screens with `toolResultScreens`. Before that plumbing the screen was reachable only by writing SDK code, which is the shape the issue called out when it asked for a good DEFAULT rather than a preset nobody can install. Listed in [the SDK index](sdk/index.md).
- **Creation** [Tool-result screens](cli/tool-result-screens.md), the CLI page for the key that turns the above on and off: the three answers (absent = the kernel's default, `[]` = none, a list = exactly those), why absent and `[]` are kept apart, the names and the refusal of an unknown one, and where in the CLI it takes effect — every registry built for a run, from one place, rather than per turn. Listed in [the CLI index](cli/index.md).
- **Update** [Tool-result screening](sdk/tool-result-screening.md) and [Tool-result screens](cli/tool-result-screens.md), for the review that sent the first cut back, and each correction is recorded rather than quietly patched. The scope no longer claims to be "connected servers" while being implemented as "provenance is set": it is `'framed'`, which is the untrusted envelope, and the page states why — an earlier predicate judged the CLI's own remote search out of scope by accident of registration while refusing a connected fetch by the same accident, and the frame is a superset of it for every result the comparison can act on. The per-tool exception is documented as a real key rather than as an SDK-only option: `{ "name": "correspondence", "passthroughTools": [...] }` inside `toolResultScreens`, with the spelling table each registration shape yields, and the `configNotices` line that names an exemption matching no tool. The CLI page's "the interactive session and its sub-agents" claim was false when it was written — `TuiContext` did not carry the key, so the surface most operators use ran the kernel default whatever the file said — and is true now, checked by a test that drives `runCli` and the App rather than asserted. The reason a refusal gives is documented as what it does: it names the tool and says the comparison was whitespace-normalised, instead of claiming a verbatim restatement it had not performed.

- **Update** The Zen catalogue lost `union-alpha` to upstream churn alone, and the gate demanded the decision instead of making it. `node scripts/generate-zen-models.mjs --check` went red on a tree nothing had touched, which is a first: models.dev deleted its entry for the id on 2026-09-18 — from `kilo/stealth`, `openrouter/stealth`, `opencode` and `opencode-go` at once, so a database edit rather than a withdrawal of the model (measured: the same fetch earlier the same day carried all four, the live document carries none, `opencode` fell from 103 models to 102 and `opencode-go` from 37 to 36). The service still serves the id and both pages still route it, on the Messages wire, so it left the roster for a reason that has nothing to do with the model: the catalogue derives limits, tool support and modalities from models.dev and never invents them, and there was nothing left to carry it with. **Recorded, not carried.** `zen/union-alpha` and `go/union-alpha` are omissions now, with a reason that says what is true — served, routed, deleted upstream on the date, back in the roster when the entry returns, and callable meanwhile by stating the wire (`protocol: 'messages'`) — the same escape hatch `omen-alpha` has. The regeneration dropped the id from both services (60 zen and 28 go became 59 and 27, the counts the published 1.0.2 ships) and the gate is green. **Three places that named it as carried are corrected**: `docs/sdk/zen.md`'s anonymous list, where it was one of the seven bundled free models, plus its new account of ids that leave the catalogue while upstream still serves them; the changeset, which had described it as one of three new models and now says plainly that nothing a consumer had stops working, because the published catalogue never carried it; and `src/__tests__/anonymous.test.ts`, whose explicit list IS the claim that the driver admits exactly these models — with a note that the fixture's Messages branch now runs for no id in that list, `union-alpha` having been the only anonymous model on that wire, and that the branch stays because the roster and not the list chooses the wire. **One claim was re-checked rather than assumed**: an omission for a model the page still documents and the service still serves does not trip the stale rule, which expires an omission only when upstream neither documents NOR serves the id, and nothing required a free-listed model to be carried.

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md)'s egress verification, for issue #507: the named-object check compared `spec.egress` to the translation with a deep-equal, and a core `NetworkPolicy` never STORES an empty rule list — `egress` is `omitempty` on the wire struct, so an object applied with `egress: []` reads back with no `egress` key at all and a merge patch cannot put one back — so `'no-network'`, whose whole translation IS that empty list, could never satisfy it: no object an operator could apply would pass, and every task create on that backend was refused (`sandbox-egress-policy-unverified`, fail closed, measured in production). `verifyEgressPolicyApplied` now reads an absent `egress` as the empty list it is, in that one comparison and nowhere else — and they are one policy rather than merely one shape, because the `policyTypes` check a few lines above has already required `'Egress'` on a core policy, which alone denies every egress. **The equivalence stops where the drift it exists to catch begins**: absent is `[]` and nothing more, so a live object carrying a rule is still refused against a translation that meant none, and a live object carrying none is still refused against a translation that meant rules — absent means deny-all, which is not what config asked for. All three are pinned as cases in `egress-policy.test.ts`, and each of the last two fails against a plausible blunter spelling of the fix (skipping the comparison whenever the translation's list is empty; substituting the expected list for an absent one) while the first fails against the pre-#507 comparison — the three mutations were run, not argued. **The round trip itself was driven against a live cluster**, because the defect IS that the round trip and the comparison disagreed: `kind` v1.37, the `no-network` manifest applied, the object read back with this backend's own `createKubernetesClient` (which answers `{"podSelector":…,"policyTypes":["Egress"]}` with no `egress` key, where `kubectl get networkpolicy -o jsonpath='{.spec.egress}'` is 0 characters and `kubectl patch --type=merge -p '{"spec":{"egress":[]}}'` leaves it that way), and the real `verifyEgressPolicyApplied` run on what came off the cluster — refused on the pre-fix build, verified after, with a live rule-bearing object and a live no-rule object both still refused. **The sibling comparisons do not have the defect, and two of those findings were measured rather than reasoned.** `decideEgressUnion`/`verifyEgressPolicyUnion` deep-equal no list at all — they reduce each policy to a per-rule verdict — and the one place absence and emptiness differ for them is `enforcesEgress`, which for a core policy reads `policyTypes`; that field is on every stored object, since one applied without it reads back with `policyTypes: ["Ingress"]`, or `["Ingress","Egress"]` when it carries a rule, so the presence fallback restates the API server's own defaulting rather than fighting it. Run live, the normalised `no-network` object reads `within` and the union check passes for it. `readCiliumEgressPolicies` reads section presence for the same field, and the normalisation is a typed-object `omitempty` rather than anything a CRD does: measured, a `SandboxTemplate` applied with `networkPolicy.egress: []` reads back with `"egress":[]` intact. The bump is `patch`: the accepted set grows by exactly the object the API server stores for the intent a `no-network` deployment already declared, nothing is removed or renamed, and the only refusal this stops is one no deployment could have been relying on — a deployment that configured `no-network` did so in order to create sandboxes.

  Three corrections to this change's own text, all from the review that approved it. The changeset claimed a host could drop an `egress.verify: 'named-object-only'` workaround, and there was no such workaround to drop: that setting gates the UNION check alone (`egressUnionVerificationEnabled` is read by `verifyUnion`, and `verifyNamedObject` runs on every create path regardless, from `index.ts`'s `create()` and from `createKubernetesWorkspace`), while the check that refused under #507 IS the named-object one — so a `no-network` host was refused under either setting, and the ways out were to configure no `egress` at all or to fall back to `deny-all`, which allows the resolver on port 53 and is not the same boundary. The refusal's clause explaining absence was stated for every branch, though it only belongs to a translation whose own list is empty: it now reads `the API server stores an empty rule list as no field at all` there and `the object carries no egress rule at all` against a translation that meant rules, which is the arm pinned by `egress-policy.test.ts`. And "a cluster never stores an empty rule list" was wider than what was measured — true of a core `NetworkPolicy`, false of the CRD this same entry measures keeping `"egress":[]` — so the page, the docblock and the changeset now name the core kind, and the comparison reads both kinds the same way because the equivalence is only ever effectual for `'no-network'`.

## 2026-09-17

- **Update** The root `README.md` onboarding flow, after RUNNING its examples instead of reading them: the key-free quickstart, the continuation snippet and the tool example each ran verbatim in a throwaway ESM project against the worktree's built `@namzu/sdk` with `tsx`, and three claims did not survive. (1) The on-disk layout was promised to include a `checkpoints/` directory, and the single-turn run writes none: the per-iteration checkpoint is taken at the end of an iteration whose tool batch was ACCEPTED and which the run continues past (`packages/sdk/src/runtime/query/iteration/phases/checkpoint.ts`, reached at `iteration/index.ts:1628`, past the review call — a rejected batch `continue`s at `:1479` and the goal-round and steering continuations reach no checkpoint phase at all), so the quickstart's run leaves `.namzu/projects/<projectId>/sessions/<sessionId>/runs/<runId>/` holding `run.json`, `messages.json`, `transcript.jsonl`, `report.md`, `audit.jsonl` and `token-budget.json`, and no `checkpoints/` at all; the run listing is its sibling at `runs/index.json`, one directory up, not a file inside the run directory. A two-iteration scripted tool loop leaves `checkpoints/` with one JSON per checkpoint — two in the run measured here, the first written before the tool batch executes and the second after it. That is why the fix is a qualification rather than a retreat: the mechanism is real and the promise was false only of the snippet it sat under. The layout paragraph now names which snippet writes that directory, and the "A run that outlives the process that started it" bullet no longer says "Each iteration writes a checkpoint", which is false of the iteration that ENDS a run for the same reason — it now says the run writes one at the end of each iteration it continues past, with a run that ends on its first turn writing none. (2) "Give it tools and the same call runs a tool loop" was false of the example under it: the provider that example reuses is scripted `responseText: 'Paris.'`, one text turn, so the registered `get_weather` tool never executed — the run's messages were `system,system,user,assistant`, no `tool` message existed and the tool's own `execute` never ran. The example now scripts the provider with `turns: [{ toolCalls: [{ name: 'get_weather', args: { city: 'Paris' } }] }, { text: 'It is 17C in Paris.' }]` and prints the run's `tool` messages, and the prose says it plainly: the mock plays its turns in order, so a tool loop is scripted rather than inferred, and the same `runAgent` call with the quickstart's single text turn returns a plain answer. Run verbatim, the corrected example prints `It is 17C in Paris.` and one tool message, `content: 'It is 17C and raining in Paris.'` — the tool's own output, so the loop demonstrably ran. (3) `The trade-off is set out in / an agent can be a directory.` was broken prose, left by `26dc90b3` when it stripped the URL out of `[an agent can be a directory](docs/sdk/directory/agent-as-a-directory.md)` and kept the connector. The page it pointed at does not exist and nothing replaced it — no page in the bundle mentions `loadDirectory`, `deriveRunOptions` or an `agent/` folder at all — so the connector is DROPPED rather than repointed and the trade-off is stated where the claim is made. It is a real trade, and it lives in the prompt builder rather than in the loader: a persona places the rendered skills section among its own sections, after constraints and tool guidance, while a plain system prompt takes precedence over a persona and gets that section immediately after the prompt (`packages/sdk/src/runtime/query/prompt.ts`). Two neighbouring claims were re-checked while there and hold: the CLI reads no `agent/` folder at all (`packages/cli/src` never names `loadDirectory`), and `importModule`, `modules: 'skip'` and the `not_loaded` marking are all real (`packages/sdk/src/directory/load.ts`). The clean-consumer half was exercised as well, with the documented prerequisites rather than a symlink: `pnpm add @namzu/sdk zod@^3` in a fresh project installed `@namzu/sdk` 41.0.0 from the registry (pnpm noting `zod 4.6.5 is available`, which is the pin the install note warns about), `pnpm add -D tsx` then `pnpm exec tsx agent.ts` reproduced `Paris.`, `end_turn` and the four-field identity, the same on-disk files arrived with no `checkpoints/`, and the corrected tool-loop example printed its two results there as well — so the quickstart, the layout correction and the corrected example all hold for the published artifact and not only for this branch's build. **Remaining audit scope, stated rather than implied**: only the root README's onboarding flow and the one page named above were walked; the other 83 pages of the bundle were not re-read against their sources, and none of the three defects here could have been caught by a gate — `node tools/check-doc-fences.mjs` does not read the root `README.md` (`FENCE_CONFORMING = ["docs"]`, so it compiles `docs/` and the package READMEs) and fence compilation establishes types anyway, never runtime results. That is why the examples were executed by hand, and why the runs above are quoted here.
- **Update** Re-read every line citation in [cancellation and timeouts](sdk/cancellation-and-timeouts.md#todays-three-layers-briefly), which opens by promising that "every claim below was checked against this branch's source at the line cited": 58 `path:LINE` references were resolved against the source and 18 of them no longer pointed at what they claim — the two the issue named (`query/index.ts:1319` → `:1369` for `provider: resilientProvider` on the `RunContext`, and `index.ts:1998` → `:2046` for `new IterationOrchestrator`) plus sixteen more. On the SDK side: `withRecovery` is built at `index.ts:1174-1187`, not `:1124-1137`; the `resilientProvider = withProviderFallback(…)` statement at `:1198-1214`, not `:1148-1163`; the ordering comment above `withRecovery` at `:1148-1167` (its last line is `// wrong.`; the `chain` array it explains starts at `:1168`), not `:1098-1117`; `boundedAdvisors` at `:1979-1988`, not `:1929-1938`; the single `remainingUntilTimeoutMs()` caller at `:2684`, not `:2603`; and the model call's own abort signal at `iteration/index.ts:887`, not `:886`. The quoted ordering sentence is at `:1159-1162`, not `:1110-1112` — the opening `The` sits at the end of `:1159` rather than at the start of the next line, which is the kind of off-by-one a range wide enough to look right will hide. On the CLI side, the session-lifetime `AbortController` is at `agent.ts:909` (aborted in `close()` at `:1059`), not `:863`/`:1013`; `operationSignal` at `:1064-1071`, not `:1018-1024`; and the signal's path from `operations.stream` to `query` at `:2968`, `:2973`, `:3896`, `:3903` with the `signal` field at `:3970`, not `:2860`, `:2865`, `:3787`, `:3794`, `:3861`. The second `zen` reader cleanup is at `client.ts:646-647`, not `:582-583`. The two `GuardCoordinator.beforeIteration` call sites the page says a full-tree grep confirms (`iteration/index.ts:474` and `:2786`) still are the only two in PRODUCTION code — `runtime/query/__tests__/resume-budget.test.ts` calls `guard.beforeIteration` directly, so the page's "only two" is production-scoped and that pre-existing sentence is left as it stands — and the rest of the page's citations resolve unchanged. `generated.at` moves to today; the page stays `draft` and no claim in it changed — only the line a reader is sent to.

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#narrowing-a-staticresolver-allowlist-ports-dns-names-tls-server-names): the config-level egress translation expands a leading-dot allowlist entry instead of emitting it verbatim (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/egress-policy.ts`, `per-sandbox-policy.ts`). `translateEgressPolicy({ kind: 'static', allowedHosts: ['.example.com'] }, 'cilium', …)` emitted `toFQDNs: [{ matchName: '.example.com' }]` — the entry VERBATIM, leading dot included. No DNS answer carries a name with a leading dot, so the object matched nothing — and nothing objected to it on the way in: the shipped admission fence is scoped to the sandbox host's ServiceAccount, so an operator applying the config-level object is not matched by it at all. `verifyEgressPolicyApplied` deep-equalled what was sent, the create reported success, and the domain and every subdomain it was asked to allow were DENIED. With `ciliumNarrowing.dnsNames` on, the DNS-visibility rule carried the same unmatched names, so the DNS proxy denied the lookup the `toFQDNs` half needs before that half could ever learn an address. `SandboxNetworkPolicy.allowedHosts` says `.example.com` is the domain AND its subdomains (`packages/sdk/src/types/sandbox/index.ts`), and `src/egress/allowlist.ts` implements exactly that for the docker backend's proxy; the per-sandbox translation already honoured it. The config-level translation now reuses `ciliumFqdnEntries` — the same helper, ONE expansion rather than two — so `.example.com` becomes `matchName: example.com` plus `matchPattern: '*.example.com'`, and `narrowedDnsVisibilityRule` carries the names that expansion admits, `matchPattern: '*.<host>.<suffix>'` under every cluster search suffix included, which is what the per-sandbox path already emitted. An entry with NO leading dot is unchanged byte for byte, with and without `dnsNames`. `expandDomains` is gone as an option: there is one translation of an entry now rather than a flag selecting between two, and `buildCiliumEgressManifest` takes a required `refusalContext` naming only which field a refusal sends its reader to, since nothing inside it can see the caller's config. The refusal of a `.domain` entry together with `tlsServerNames` still fires on both paths and was not weakened; its sentence is now the same one on both, because the entry becomes a name plus a `*.domain` pattern whichever translation emits it, so its remedy ("leave `tlsServerNames` off for a domain list") is a real repair on both — and `KubernetesNetworkPolicyHostError`'s `stateHostnameGrammar` option, whose only caller was the config-level refusal, is removed with it. The byte-pin test that asserted the malformed `matchName: '.example.com'` is corrected to the expanded pair, joined by a control proving a non-dotted host's bytes did not move. The UNION check stopped judging the named object's own rules, because with the expansion it refused the very object the fix tells an operator to apply: `egressAllowance` records a `toFQDNs` entry by its `matchName` alone, the expansion's second entry is a `matchPattern` with none, so the named object read as `widens-egress` on every `create()` however many times the manifest was re-applied — under the default `egress.verify: 'union'`, permanently. What is left to `verifyEgressPolicyApplied` is the document built from the named object's own `spec` — the thing check ONE compared, field by field, to the translation on that same create path — and never an `item.specs` entry: that comparison reads `spec.podSelector`/`spec.endpointSelector`, `spec.policyTypes` and `spec.egress`, and it now REFUSES a live object carrying a `specs` list outright, because a `CiliumNetworkPolicy` carries EITHER one `spec` or a `specs` list, a rule in either one enforces, and reporting a match on half of what an object enforces is the one answer that check must not give (the shipped admission fence refuses a `specs` list for the same reason). So a `specs` entry is judged by the union rule like any other object's rules — refused by name when it widens, and a `specs` entry riding beside an exactly-matching `spec` no longer passes either check. The named object still decides the one thing the exact comparison cannot answer: whether it puts this pod in egress default-deny, so a deployment whose only applied policy is that object still creates while a second policy that allows more is still refused by name. And the hostname grammar `allowedHosts` entries are held to — a hostname, or a leading dot and a domain with at least two labels — ran only on the per-sandbox path, where it is reached through `normalizeHost`; the config-level translation passed every entry straight through, so `'.com'`, `'.'`, `'..example.com'`, `'*'` and an IP literal were emitted. Before the expansion those emitted a `matchName` that matched nothing, i.e. a fail-closed no-op; with it, `'.com'` would emit `matchPattern: '*.com'` and grant every name under a public suffix, silently, on a path the shipped admission fence does not cover.
