The check now runs in the shared builder both translations pass through, so the same entries are refused by name, with `KubernetesNetworkPolicyHostError` and nothing emitted, on either path — the per-sandbox writer still calls it first as well, before it reads the fence. **Stays unproven here**: that a Cilium L7 proxy admits `example.com` and its subdomains and nothing else under the emitted pair — this repository has no cluster with a Cilium data plane, and the `kind` cluster its Kubernetes tests use enforces no policy at all, so the translation is proved and the ENFORCEMENT of it is not.
- **Update** Complete the [container hardening baseline](../packages/sandbox/README.md#container-hardening-baseline) for `container:docker`, and stop describing one this package did not have (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/docker/index.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/docker/__tests__/hardening.test.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/index.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/index.test.ts`, `packages/sandbox/worker/Dockerfile`). The backend's confinement was two flags — `--cap-drop=ALL` and `--security-opt=no-new-privileges` — beside resource bounds the host sets per call, and against the published container-hardening guidance it was four controls of about ten: CPU had no knob at all, every path inside the container was writable, and whether another container could join the container's IPC namespace was whatever the host daemon's `default-ipc-mode` happened to be. The argv is now built by an exported pure function, `buildDockerRunArgs`, assembled from `renderHardeningArgs` and `renderWritableRootfsArgs`, because everything downstream of it needs a running Docker daemon and a confinement flag that never reached the argv could only be caught by an operator noticing its effect missing in production; `packages/sandbox/src/backends/docker/__tests__/hardening.test.ts` pins the whole array for a default config, refuses to let `--privileged`, a `cap-add` or a `seccomp=` appear in it, and covers the three bounds, `--user`, the proxy environment and the image's position. **What it applies.** `--ipc private` makes this container's IPC namespace un-joinable, and the sentence before this one said something else — that omitting the flag on a host whose `daemon.json` sets `default-ipc-mode: shareable` merges every container on that host into one namespace. It does not: moby runs `private`, `shareable` and `none` through the SAME branch (`daemon/oci_linux.go`, `WithNamespaces`), so `shareable` already gives each container a namespace of its own, and what it leaves on top is what docker's run reference calls "a possibility to share it with other containers" — `--ipc container:<name>`, which joins another container's namespace and which is gated on the target having a shared-memory directory to enter — `daemon.getIPCContainer` resolves the target and checks its `ShmPath` — which a container created `private` does not have. The reachability is the real consequence, and it is the one stated here now: on such a host this sandbox's namespace, and the System V shared memory, semaphores and message queues namespaced with it, were joinable by name from anything else able to create a container against the same daemon, and the flag removes that and puts the answer in the argv rather than in `daemon.json` (`none` is deliberately not used: it takes `/dev/shm` away, which chromium needs for every renderer). `--read-only` makes the image not a place the workload writes, with the paths that must stay writable mounted `--tmpfs`: `/tmp`, `/var/tmp`, `/workspace` and `/home/namzu`, each reasoned about in the file from the reference image's own Dockerfile — the doc-gen toolchain (`LibreOffice` refuses a headless conversion without a writable `~/.config/libreoffice`, matplotlib builds a font cache, `pip install --user` needs `~/.local`), the image's `WORKDIR` chowned to `namzu`, and the temp directories `TMPDIR` and the `mkstemp` conventions fall back to. Measured against moby: docker mounts every user `--tmpfs` from `noexec,nosuid,nodev` and appends the caller's options, so a plain `--tmpfs /tmp` is **noexec** — every scratch mount here therefore passes `exec` explicitly, or a workload that compiles something into `/tmp` and runs it would meet `Permission denied` on a file that is plainly executable. A path the layout already mounts is skipped rather than mounted twice (docker refuses two mounts at one destination, and the host's own bind is the one that must win) while a path the HOST names that the layout also mounts is refused, because there the two requests contradict each other. Both comparisons are made against the spelling moby itself reduces a destination to, because nothing else normalises the layout's own paths: `resolveLayout` fills in defaults and compares spellings as written, so a layout that wrote `/tmp/`, `//tmp` or `/tmp/.` for one of its mounts would have produced both a `--tmpfs /tmp:...` and a bind at the same directory, which the daemon cleans into one destination and refuses at create with `Duplicate mount point: /tmp` — the spawn-time failure the guard exists to prevent, on the one path no test in this repository can reach. The host-named paths need no such cleaning: a spelling that is not a normalised absolute path is refused outright, `/` and every empty, `.` or `..` segment included. A new `cpuLimit` renders `--cpus` and is deliberately opt-in with no default, like `memoryLimitMb` and `maxProcesses`: a number chosen here would throttle a run that finishes inside its timeout today. **What is deliberately absent**, with the reason in the same docblock: a seccomp profile (docker applies its built-in one to every container and nothing here asks for anything looser, so what is missing is a *tighter* profile, and a hand-written one cannot be verified in this repository against the reference image's toolchain — a profile that blocks a syscall chromium or LibreOffice needs breaks the sandbox, which is worse than the gap it closes; a host that wants one sets `seccomp-profile` in `daemon.json`); `--userns-remap` (a daemon property, not a `docker run` flag — a container only chooses among the namespaces the daemon has already made (`--userns=host|private`), so whether a remapped mapping exists is settled before this argv is read); and `--user`, unset by default because it OVERRIDES the image's own choice, which for the image this package ships is already `USER namzu`. **Two sentences that were not true.** `src/index.ts` opened by promising the container tier "seccomp on, tmpfs workdir, no network unless asked" on behalf of both of its backends, and none of the three was a property this package established: nothing here passes a seccomp flag (a daemon's own profile is what filters syscalls), the docker tier's working directory is the layout's `outputs` bind mount, and which network a container is attached to is decided per backend. It now says what confines a workload inside the container is applied by each backend and documented where it is applied, naming the docker baseline and the ACI pool's profile. `worker/Dockerfile`'s trust model line said "Host mounts `/workspace` writable to that uid", which was true of neither the backend's default layout (which binds `outputs` and `scratch` under `/mnt/user-data`) nor a read-only rootfs; it now says the uid was never what made `/workspace` writable. **Third-party verification is still missing**: the writable set was derived by reading the image, and `packages/sandbox/src/backends/docker/__tests__/hardening.test.ts` proves the argv and not the running container. The new test caught a real bug on its first run: with `--read-only` sitting in the unconditional flag array, `readOnlyRootfs: false` was accepted and ignored, which is the "control accepted and not applied" failure this backend refuses everywhere else. `.changeset/sandbox-docker-hardening-baseline.md`, **major** for `@namzu/sandbox` — two defaults changed in ways a workload that works today can fail under (a write inside the container outside the four scratch paths and the layout's binds, most realistically the `HOME` of an image whose user is not `namzu`, now fails with `EROFS`; and those four paths are now RAM rather than the container's writable layer, so a temp file larger than half the host's RAM or than `--memory` fails with `ENOSPC` where spilling to disk used to succeed; the IPC namespace is no longer joinable by name from elsewhere on the host), and the changeset names both remedies (`writableRootfsPaths`, or `readOnlyRootfs: false`, which gives up the read-only root filesystem and only that — `--ipc private` is applied whatever it says; the changeset gives the disk-backed `layout.scratch` bind and a `TMPDIR` pointing at it as the remedy to reach for first, since it keeps the baseline). Nothing here touches `@namzu/sdk`: `cpuLimit` lives on `ContainerBackendConfig` rather than the tier-agnostic per-call options, because the ACI and kubernetes backends cannot apply a per-sandbox CPU limit any more than they can apply the memory and process ones, and a per-call field would have to be refused by both.

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#flushing-a-workspaces-writes-before-its-pod-stops): a workspace's writes are now put on its disk on purpose, rather than left to whatever the guest kernel had written back when the pod stopped (`packages/sandbox/agent/agent.cjs`, `k8s/entrypoint.sh`, `k8s/manifests/sandboxtemplate-workspace.yaml`, `src/backends/kubernetes/workspace.ts`, `transport.ts`, `src/backends/firecracker/protocol.ts`). Nothing in the agent or the backend ever called `sync`, `syncfs` or `fsync`: `suspend()` patched the pod away and waited for it to stop, and both the code and this page treated that wait as the point the disk was quiesced — a stopped pod only means nothing is writing any more. The new section documents the three independent paths the fix ships, because any one of them can be the one that does not fire on a given runtime: the host sends a `flush` op (`syncfs(2)` over the workspace mount) and waits for it BEFORE the `Suspended` patch; the workspace pod runs `/entrypoint.sh prestop` as a `preStop` hook, which syncs, signals pid 1 and waits for it; and the agent's own `SIGTERM` handler stops accepting connections, stops every process it is running through the same routine `quiesce()` uses, syncs and exits 0 under `NAMZU_AGENT_SHUTDOWN_DEADLINE_MS`. `writeFile`'s own contract changed with it and is documented where the part protocol is: a reply of `ok` now means the bytes are on the device (temp sibling, `fsync`, rename, directory `fsync`), so every write of any size is atomic at the target and durable when it answers, and a failed write leaves the previous contents rather than a truncated file. The target's mode is carried onto the replacement on BOTH paths, and that half is a repair rather than a new promise: the single-frame path had applied it since it was made durable, while the part path — renaming since #475 — created its temp file and renamed it with no `chmod` at all, so a 0755 script came back 0644 with the reply still saying `ok` and the bytes still right, and a 0600 file came back 0644 the same way. Both paths now carry it, and a mode that cannot be applied fails the write instead of replacing a file with one whose permissions differ from the file it replaced. Also recorded: the rule that decides which flush failures stop a suspend — only a guest that ANSWERED and could not confirm (`KubernetesFlushUnconfirmedError`, no patch sent), while an image that cannot flush (`onFlushUnsupported`) and a guest that cannot be reached to be asked (`onFlushUnreachable`, new) are reported and the suspend goes ahead, because refusing there would take the lifecycle verb away from exactly the crashed, fenced or OOM-killed workspace it is needed for; that a guest advertises `flush` only if it has the program to run one, so an image stripped of coreutils degrades instead of refusing every suspend forever; `suspend({ flush: false })` and the standalone `suspendKubernetesWorkspace`'s refusal of an explicit `flush: true`; what replacing rather than overwriting costs a single-frame `writeFile` (hard links broken, the replacement owned by the agent's uid, `ENAMETOOLONG` past a ~200-character basename, a containing directory that must now be writable, and a briefly visible `.part` sibling); the `FailedPreStopHook` warning an operator sees on every stop that worked, since the hook ends the pid namespace's own init and is SIGKILLed with it; that the hook's wait is DERIVED from `NAMZU_AGENT_SHUTDOWN_DEADLINE_MS` (`ceil(ms / 1000) + 2` seconds) rather than fixed, because the kubelet signals pid 1 only once the hook returns and a shorter wait would cap the agent's drain at the hook's number, and that the agent's deadline is correspondingly its only bound — a repeat `SIGTERM` is logged and ignored; that `suspend({ flush: { timeoutMs } })` raises what the guest may spend inside the `syncfs`, and that a `destroy()` joining a `suspend({ flush: false })` already in flight is refused rather than joined; and the shipped `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` moving 5 → 30 — stated plainly as a BUDGET nobody has measured (15s of it is the agent's own bound), with the two bounds that hold it in place and the instruction to measure it on the operator's own runtime. Row 3 of the acceptance table now names what `suspend-resume.mjs` measures, including the suspend's duration, and the section ends with what no test in this repo can prove: the device itself, whether a kubelet runs the `preStop` hook under a VM runtime class, and the pathology that motivated the work. Two things the hook's own documentation now states outright: it reads `/proc/<pid>/comm` and signals only a process named like the init this image starts (`NAMZU_PRESTOP_INIT_NAME` renames it), because `kill` is a shell builtin and the pid it defaults to is 1; and its `sync -f` runs inside the grace period and is unbounded, so the number to measure is `sync-time + NAMZU_AGENT_SHUTDOWN_DEADLINE_MS`. The TASK template's half of the same pair is recorded too: `sandboxtemplate-task.yaml` keeps its 5 s grace period and now sets `NAMZU_AGENT_SHUTDOWN_DEADLINE_MS: 3000` inside it, so the agent's own bound expires before the kubelet's `SIGKILL` rather than 10 s after it — its comment had justified the 5 s by the prompt `process.exit(0)`-on-signal handler this change replaced, and now describes the bounded drain that exists — and the template deliberately carries no `preStop` hook, because the wait a hook would derive from a 3000 ms deadline (`ceil(3000 / 1000) + 2` = 5 s) is that entire grace period. `manifests.test.ts` scans every shipped template that sets a deadline and asserts the pair against the manifests themselves (the deadline inside the grace period; the hook's derived wait inside it wherever a hook ships), walking `.yml` as well as `.yaml` so a future overlay cannot sit outside the scan.
- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#per-sandbox-egress-setnetworkpolicy-on-a-live-sandbox): the egress translation refuses one combination it used to emit — a `.domain` allowlist entry together with `tlsServerNames` — with `KubernetesNetworkPolicyHostError` and nothing written (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/egress-policy.ts`, `per-sandbox-policy.ts`). A TLS server name is one exact SNI value a handshake presents, while `.example.com` means that domain and every subdomain of it, so the `serverNames` the option attaches means something the entry does not: `['.example.com']` is not a name any handshake presents and `['example.com']` denies every subdomain the same rule's `toFQDNs` half admits. The object would be admitted by the shipped fence and read back deep-equal to what was sent, so `setNetworkPolicy` would have resolved and reported success while denying the domain it was asked to allow. The refusal sits in the shared translation, so it covers the config-level `ciliumNarrowing` allowlist as well as `perSandbox.narrowing`, and the per-sandbox writer makes the same check before it reads the fence. A fence read the API server REFUSES — `401`/`403`, which is what applying the namespaced `Role` without the `ClusterRole` in `k8s/manifests/rbac-per-sandbox-egress.yaml` produces, since both admission objects are cluster-scoped — is now `KubernetesAdmissionFenceUnreadableError` instead of a report that the fence is missing, because the two send an operator to different files. The page and `packages/sandbox/k8s/README.md` also state two bounds the shipped fence enforces more precisely than they did: the selector KEY is pinned alongside the owner's name as the value (the value alone let a claim named `namzu-task` select on the shared template label), and the DELETE arm is bounded by the `namzu-sbx-` prefix alone — the operator's baseline, not one sandbox's policy from another. The refusal's own message was made path-aware by that change, because the entry was then a different thing on each path and one sentence could not be true of both: the per-sandbox translation expands `.example.com` into a name plus a `*.example.com` pattern, so its message said that and its remedy ("list the exact hosts, or leave `tlsServerNames` off for a domain list") was a real repair there, while the CONFIG-level translation then expanded nothing — the entry reached the object as the literal `matchName: '.example.com'`, which no DNS answer carries, so it admitted nothing with the option on or off and `serverNames` on top of it was a second, independent denial — and its message said that instead of offering a remedy that was not one. Which values are refused is unchanged; only what a refused caller is told. The raw `.domain` byte emission the config-level translation had always produced was left exactly as it was, a defect deferred to its own change, which is the first entry under this date.

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#per-sandbox-egress-setnetworkpolicy-on-a-live-sandbox): `Sandbox.setNetworkPolicy` is implemented on Kubernetes sandboxes when the backend declares `config.egress.perSandbox` (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/per-sandbox-policy.ts`, `egress-policy.ts`, `sandbox.ts`, `index.ts`, and the two opt-in manifests `k8s/manifests/validatingadmissionpolicy-cilium.yaml` and `k8s/manifests/rbac-per-sandbox-egress.yaml`). Egress here was one policy for every sandbox a backend produced, so the SDK's live per-sandbox contract — narrow this run's egress after fetching what it is about to execute — was omitted, and a per-tenant host list cost an operator-applied policy, a template and a warm pool of its own. Each call now writes one `CiliumNetworkPolicy` for that sandbox alone: named after the `SandboxClaim`'s uid, selecting one per-sandbox pod label carried in the same claim-time `additionalPodMetadata.labels` map the egress profile travels in and confirmed on the bound pod before the sandbox is handed back, owned by the claim through `ownerReferences` so `destroy()` lets the cluster collect it, and resolving only once a read-back deep-equals the translation through the comparator the named-object check already used; `setNetworkPolicy([])` deletes that one object and leaves the configured baseline in force, and is fenced like every other write rather than exempted for being a narrowing. Because cluster policies union, a per-sandbox list ADDS to what `egress.policy` translated to and is the pod's whole boundary only under a denying baseline — the page says so, and says that neither combination is refused. The per-sandbox selector label's value is unique per acquire and is therefore excluded from the key of both network-verification memos, which are keyed on a pod's label set: carrying it there would have turned a once-per-backend namespace-wide policy enumeration into one per sandbox and left an entry behind for every sandbox a host ever produced. The new section documents why the method is conditionally PRESENT rather than always present and throwing, the two operator prerequisites (the admission fence the backend proves before its first write, and the policy-write RBAC the default Role deliberately withholds), and what the fence refuses — including the two bounds that carry the weight: the selector's single label has to hold the OWNER's own name (one label is otherwise exactly what selects every sandbox pod in the namespace) and every `toFQDNs` entry has to name something (`matchPattern: '*'` is a `toFQDNs` rule and is not an allowlist), so `*`, `*.*` and `*.com` are refused by the fence and by the writer alike. The capability table's `setNetworkPolicy` row moves from **Omitted** to implemented-when-configured, the RBAC section gains the opt-in grant, and [what no egress test here can prove](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#what-no-egress-test-here-can-prove) records both halves honestly: the object lifecycle, the ownerReference garbage collection, every fence refusal and the 51-concurrent-policy run were measured on a local single-node cluster carrying the upstream CRD with no data plane, and ENFORCEMENT — the issue's "the registry answers and example.com does not" — was not measured anywhere in this repository. Two things the first pass left out are recorded now. The option is refused on the WORKSPACE entry point: `createKubernetesWorkspace` throws `KubernetesWorkspacePerSandboxEgressConfigError` — a new export — before the first request when `config.egress.perSandbox` is set, because a `KubernetesWorkspace` handle never carries `setNetworkPolicy`, and a configuration accepted there would declare a capability nothing serves. And `dnsNames` narrowing on the per-sandbox path covers an expanded entry's subdomains: `.example.com` emits `matchPattern: '*.<host>.<suffix>'` beside `<host>.<suffix>` for every cluster search suffix, since a guest's first lookup of `a.example.com` under the default `ndots: 5` tries those suffixes and a refused lookup can fail the whole resolution — both halves of that are new, the exact-host branch emitting `matchName`s and never a pattern.

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#a-narrower-role-for-a-host-that-only-claims): a second, deliberately narrower RBAC grant ships for a host that only claims from a warm pool (`packages/sandbox/k8s/manifests/rbac-claimant.yaml`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/rbac.ts`, `packages/sandbox/k8s/__tests__/manifests.test.ts`). `rbac.yaml`'s `sandboxes: create` is a privilege-escalation path rather than a convenience in a namespace that also has to run the privileged workspace template — any identity holding it can POST a `Sandbox` whose podTemplate is privileged, hostPath-mounting or on another `RuntimeClass` — and RBAC decides per verb, never per object shape, so the only way to take that away from a host that does not need it is to not grant it. The claimant Role keeps `sandboxclaims` create/get/list/patch/delete, `sandboxes` get, `sandboxwarmpools` get, `pods` get/list and get/list on both policy resources, and withholds every `sandboxes` write, every write on a policy resource, and the `sandboxtemplates`/`persistentvolumeclaims` reads — each of those because the pool-only path has no call site that issues it (the template read sits in the pool-LESS branch of the create body). The list is not prose: the backend exports `KUBERNETES_CLAIMANT_RBAC_RULES` from the package root, and `manifests.test.ts` pins it in both directions. It parses both Role files and asserts the claimant Role grants exactly that constant and stays a strict subset of the wider Role's grants; it asserts the constant is frozen one level deeper than the array, so a plain-JS consumer cannot rewrite a rule in place; and it reads the pool-only path's own sources (`src/backends/kubernetes/{index,objects,ingress-policy,egress-policy}.ts`), resolving every literal `client.request('<METHOD>', …)` in them to the `(apiGroup, resource, verb)` triple the path builder it goes through addresses and failing on any request it cannot resolve — an undeclared builder, a builder call with anything appended to it (a `pods/log`-style subresource is authorized separately from its parent, and the concatenated spelling used to resolve as the parent), a NAME the source writes a second path to — the guard reads a plain assignment wherever it is written, not only where it opens a statement, plus a compound one, a `for (path of …)` binding and a destructuring target — as a `const`/`let`/`var`-declared one, the same appended-subresource escape in the spelling that reaches a request through a variable rather than as the argument, or as a wrapper's own path parameter, which is resolved through its call sites — the declared spelling used to resolve from the initializer, and the parameter spelling from the call sites, each reporting the parent's triple while the request sent the subresource, a request in no function body the scan can match, or a path that is a parameter of a body whose call sites it has not been told about — the declaration it reads that parameter out of is found by the same reader the scan attributes bodies with, so `function NAME(…)` and `const NAME = … =>` are the same declaration to it — so a second wrapper declares itself instead of resolving through the function it sits under. The file set is pinned the same way: any other `.ts` under `src/backends/kubernetes/` that reaches the client — calling `client.request(…)` itself in either spelling the scan reads, the literal one or a computed `client['request']`, or naming `listPolicies`, the one wrapper the scan knows, which is how a file can issue a request with no `.request` in it; every half read on text with its comments blanked — fails the test until it is scanned or declared off the path with its reason, so the four names cannot stop covering the path by staying four names. Two blind spots in that mechanism were closed rather than written down as limits: a wrapper declared in the arrow form was in no declaration the wrapper map's lookup could find, so its call sites resolved to nothing in silence (53/53 green, where the same plant written with the `function` keyword failed by name), and a file whose only route to the client is `client['request'](…)` was required to be declared by nobody, because the predicate's literal half has no dot to match — the spelling the scan otherwise refuses by line. A third was closed the same way rather than listed: the scan used to resolve a name through its `const`/`let`/`var` initializer and read NO other write to it, so a `path` initialized from `podCollectionPath(namespace)` and then reassigned to that same builder's `pods/log` subresource resolved as `pods list` while the request reached a resource RBAC authorizes separately — the argument spelling of that concatenation failed by name and the assigned spelling passed all 53 cases; a name the source writes a second path to now fails by name, naming the file, the line and the write, whichever half would have resolved it, and the guard reads four spellings — a plain assignment `path = …` WHEREVER it is written (the branch whose assignment never opens a statement included), a compound one, a `for (path of …)` loop binding and a destructuring target naming it — while stepping over the `const`/`let`/`var` declaration it resolves a name through. So a verb added to the pool-only path IN CODE, with neither the constant nor the manifest touched, fails a test naming the resource, the file and the line, instead of surfacing as a `403` in a host's log — and a rule the code never issues fails it from the other side, because the grant would then be wider than the path. What it does not see: the files declared off the path (`workspace.ts`, `k8s-client.ts`, `transport.ts`) are never resolved to triples, so a request there is unmeasured — a wrapper call site in one of them included, since call sites are read from the scanned files only; a wrapper reached by ANOTHER name is outside that file predicate, which matches the wrapper's name and not its identity, so a file importing a re-export of `listPolicies` — or calling a helper one level further out that calls it — is neither scanned nor required to be declared; a path arriving through an expression-bodied arrow's parameter is outside what it matches; a body it could not register — a class method, or an object literal's shorthand method, declared inside one of the bodies it reads — is attributed to the body that encloses it, so the nested body's own parameters are invisible; the assignment guard is by name in the file it resolves in rather than by scope, so a binding it registers nowhere shadows nothing there — a destructuring parameter (`function f({ path })`), a `catch (path)`, a write made to an exported binding from another module — and an assignment to a same-named local in an unrelated function of the same file refuses the request too, a false refusal rather than an escape; a parameter carrying a default (`function f(path = …)`, `function f({ path } = {})`) is read by that guard and refuses the request; and a destructuring pattern nested inside another (`({ a: { path } } = …)`) is read by neither spelling, which is measured green. That list is the SHORT form, not the list: it is carried in full in the header of `packages/sandbox/k8s/__tests__/manifests.test.ts`, which also holds the bullet `a path assembled by an operation the scan does not model`. And it contacts no API server. The existing Role is unchanged and nothing differs for an operator unless they bind a ServiceAccount to the new one. The direct-create `ValidatingAdmissionPolicy` example the issue asked for is deliberately NOT in this change: it lands with the per-sandbox capability work, written against this repository's first admission-policy manifest, so there is one model to copy rather than two. Neither kind-level acceptance claim is measured — no host has been bound to the Role against a cluster — and the manifest's own header says so.

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#a-resume-can-bring-the-current-pod-template-with-it): a workspace can now be woken onto the `SandboxTemplate` as it stands today, keeping its disk (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/workspace.ts`, `objects.ts`, `index.ts`). A `Sandbox` carries its own copy of `spec.podTemplate`, taken once in the create `POST`, and agent-sandbox builds every replacement pod from that copy — so a workspace kept for weeks ran the pod spec it was created with, and a new image tag, a memory limit, a `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` or an env entry reached only workspaces created after the edit. The sharp case is a release that changes the agent's wire protocol: every session start runs the privilege probe through an `exec`, the shared execution controller refuses a reservation whose `protocolVersion` is not the host's, and so every adopt and every resume of a workspace pinning the old image failed with `destroy({ deleteDisk: true })` — which takes the disk — as the only exit. `refreshPodTemplate` is opt-in on both entry points (`resume({ refreshPodTemplate: true })`, and `createKubernetesWorkspace(config, { …, refreshPodTemplate: true })` for a restarted host that has no handle) and is honoured on exactly one transition, Suspended → Running. The new section documents the single `application/json-patch+json` body that does it — `test /spec/operatingMode == "Suspended"`, then the annotations, then `/spec/podTemplate`, then `/spec/operatingMode` — why it is a JSON Patch rather than the merge patch every other lifecycle write sends (a merge patch recurses into maps, so a `nodeSelector` entry the template dropped would survive on the object), and that a configured holder epoch's `test` rides in that same body rather than in a second request. It also states what the refresh will not do: never a Running `Sandbox`, because v1.0.2 does not rewrite a pod that already exists, so an adopt that finds it Running and a `test` that loses to another process's resume both bind the pod that is there unchanged and neither is retried; never the disk, because `spec.volumeClaimTemplates` is CEL-immutable and is not in the patch, with two checks before anything is sent (both `KubernetesWorkspaceDiskError`, workspace left `Suspended`) — that the refreshed pod template still claims **this** workspace's disk through `volumeDevices`, and that the template declares no disk this workspace does not have, the second being the one only a refresh can get wrong, since adding a `volumeClaimTemplates` entry with its matching `volumeDevices` entry is a valid edit a new workspace would honour and an existing one cannot have; and never another template, because the label refusal is unconditional while the `runtimeClassName` refusal is lifted only for a call whose patch actually lands and therefore writes the configured class. Two new handle fields, `templateRevision` and `templateCurrent`, read off a new `sandbox.namzu.ai/pod-template-hash` annotation that every create `POST` now stamps, so a host can schedule the suspend-and-resume itself instead of discovering the drift when a release makes the stored image fail every start; a workspace created before the annotation existed reports `undefined` and `false`, because unknown is not current. The page keeps and cites its existing statement that nothing is patched to make a standing object match, now qualified by the explicit opt-in, and `packages/sandbox/k8s/README.md` gains the operator half: a new image tag does not reach an existing workspace on its own. On an object that carries no `metadata.annotations` at all — only a workspace created before this release, since every create now stamps at least the revision — the shared patch builder has to add the map WHOLE, which replaces whatever is there; that operation is now conditioned on `/metadata/resourceVersion` whether or not the call carries a holder epoch, so a refresh cannot erase an annotation another writer stamped in the window (a suspend carrying an epoch onto an already-suspended object writes `metadata` and leaves `spec` alone, which the operating-mode clause cannot see). A refused patch is told apart from a wrong one by re-reading the object, and that re-read can find nothing at all: a workspace deleted in that window surfaces as the gone object it is, on both entry points, rather than as the "another process resumed it first" outcome, which would have sent the caller on to bind a pod that does not exist. **Stays unproven here**: that the sandbox controller builds the replacement pod from `spec.podTemplate` AS REWRITTEN — upstream behaviour, taken from its source; the run that would settle it needs neither a disk nor a workspace (a bare `Sandbox` with any pod template, suspended, sent exactly this patch with a changed image tag, a changed grace period and one `nodeSelector` key dropped, and the replacement pod's own spec read back), and it was attempted and could not be made, because the environment this was written in refuses cluster writes; the `test` clause bounds the cost of the premise being wrong, since a refresh that never reaches the pod is a missed refresh rather than a wrong write, but `templateCurrent` would then report a workspace as current while its pod ran the old spec — and, for the separate reason that it needs block storage, the disk-preserving round trip through a real block-mode PVC (file digests match, PVC uid unchanged). No new RBAC and no guest protocol change; without the option every request is what it always was.

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#what-an-acquire-refuses-with): a refused acquire now says WHY. `create()` rejects with `KubernetesAcquireError` carrying one of seven reasons (`api-unreachable`, `api-timeout`, `forbidden`, `claim-rejected`, `capacity`, `image-pull`, `not-ready`), a `retryable` flag and the original failure as its `cause` (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/index.ts`). A burst past node capacity and an API outage used to be the same plain `Error`, separable only by matching message text. Three behaviours change with it, and the new "What an acquire refuses with" subsection under "Two acquire paths" describes each. (1) A claim the controller has already REFUSED fails on the first read rather than after the full `readyTimeoutMs`: four `status.conditions[Ready]` reasons mean decided rather than not-yet — `WarmPoolNotFound`, `TemplateNotFound`, `InvalidMetadata`, `EnvVarsInjectionRejected` — read off a live agent-sandbox v1.0.2 controller on Kubernetes v1.37.0 rather than copied from a changelog, and an unrecognised reason still falls through to the deadline. Measured: a claim naming a missing warm pool is refused in 233 ms against a 60 000 ms budget, with the claim deleted behind it. (2) A readiness `GET` that fails transiently — a connect failure, an `apiRequestTimeoutMs` expiry, a 429, a 5xx — is retried INSIDE the readiness deadline honouring `Retry-After`, so a `create()` against a failing API server that used to reject in milliseconds now rejects after the whole budget; the create `POST` is never retried, because it is not idempotent. (3) `capacity` and `image-pull` come from one pod `GET` made only after the budget has gone and before the cleanup `DELETE`, so the healthy path reads nothing it did not read before, and a pod condition the cluster published outranks an API failure the poll had already recovered from. Fail-fast is a claim-path property — the four reasons are `SandboxClaim` conditions, so a pool-less `Sandbox` still waits out its budget, though its pod diagnosis is made either way. The page also records that a failure none of the seven reasons honestly describes travels out as itself, and that the API client's own classes — `KubernetesApiError` (with `status`, `retryAfterMs` and a `connect`/`status` transport), `KubernetesCredentialError`, `KubernetesAlreadyGoneError`, `KubernetesConflictError` — plus `ReadinessPollTimeout` and the three egress refusals are now exported from the package root, which is what makes catching by class possible at all.
- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#quiescing-the-guest-before-a-capture): a workspace's guest can now be quiesced — every process it is running stopped, with the agent left up to read the disk through — by the new additive `quiesce` agent op and `KubernetesWorkspace.quiesce()` (`packages/sandbox/agent/agent.cjs`, `src/backends/kubernetes/transport.ts`, `workspace.ts`, `src/backends/firecracker/protocol.ts`). `suspend()` promises a quiesced disk and only ever kept that promise after the pod had stopped, at which point nothing can read the disk; and what it reaches on its own is narrower than it looks — the terminals THAT handle returned plus an execution cancelled by id, so a terminal another host process opened, an `exec` already in flight, and a program that moved into a session of its own with `setsid` and was then reparented away from the agent all kept writing into the drain. The op marks every running execution BEFORE it signals anything (without the mark, a group leader dying before the rest of its group makes the execution's close handler fence the agent, which would then refuse the very capture the quiesce was performed for — the subtlest requirement in the change), scans `/proc` rather than the agent's own children, skips PID 1, itself, its own kernel session and zombies, and signals in rounds — `SIGTERM`, `graceMs`, `SIGKILL` on what is left — until a pass finds nothing, which is also what makes a second quiesce answer with an empty list. A process still present after `SIGKILL` rejects with `KubernetesQuiesceUnconfirmedError` naming its pid rather than resolving optimistically; while the op runs, every op that would start a process answers `quiesce_in_progress` while `healthz`, `cancel-execution`, `list-sessions`, `read-file`, `read-file-stream`, `write-file` and `tcp-connect` are still served. `suspend({ quiesce: true })` and `destroy({ quiesce: true })` run it after `reapTerminals()` and before the `Suspended` patch — it cannot run later, since `admit` refuses every call the moment the state leaves `running` — and a quiesce that cannot be confirmed sends **no patch**, leaving the workspace running and admitting calls; the standalone `suspendKubernetesWorkspace` refuses the option rather than ignoring it, because it never dials the agent. The option is REFUSED wherever it cannot be honoured rather than accepted and dropped, and that rule reaches three more places: it lives on the new `KubernetesWorkspaceSuspendOptions` rather than on the shared `KubernetesWorkspaceTransitionOptions`, so `resume()`, `refresh()`, `listKubernetesWorkspaces` and `deleteKubernetesWorkspace` do not accept it at all; a `suspend({ quiesce: true })` that arrives while a suspend WITHOUT a quiesce is already in flight is rejected with `KubernetesQuiesceUnconfirmedError` (`reason: 'suspend_already_in_flight'`) instead of joining that transition, which is on its way to patching over a guest nothing stopped and admits nothing once its state leaves `running`, while a caller the flight already satisfies joins it as before and one quiesce is performed between them; and a guest that narrowed its scan reaches the one caller that cannot read the report — `suspend({ quiesce: true })` answers `void` — through the new `KubernetesWorkspaceOptions.onQuiesceNarrowed`. The page documents the `graceMs`-versus-`terminationGracePeriodSeconds` distinction (different clocks, different budgets; `graceMs` is clamped below `NAMZU_AGENT_CANCEL_CONFIRM_TIMEOUT_MS` because a marked execution's close handler stops waiting there) and the scan's own scope: the general scan covers the guest's PID namespace, which in a pod is the container and nothing else, and the agent performs it only when it is the init of that namespace or was started by it — `k8s/entrypoint.sh` makes `tini` PID 1 and the agent its child — while an agent that is neither narrows itself to the kernel sessions its registries own and reports `scope: 'owned-sessions'` instead of being silently weaker. The guest wire protocol version is unchanged; `quiesce` is advertised in `healthz` and a host only ever sends it to a guest that advertised it, with an explicit `quiesce()` against an older image refused by class and a `suspend({ quiesce: true })` against one suspending as it always did and reporting the gap through the new `KubernetesWorkspaceOptions.onQuiesceUnsupported`. New test `backends/kubernetes/__tests__/workspace-quiesce.test.ts` runs the real `agent.cjs` inside a PID namespace of its own (`unshare --user --pid --fork --mount-proc`) so the general scan is proved on real processes without touching the test host: a `setsid` writer that outlives its terminal is stopped and its file stops growing, a `trap '' TERM` program is escalated to `SIGKILL` and reported with it, an in-flight `exec` whose group leader dies first resolves with a signal and the agent does NOT retire, sessions read `exited` rather than detached afterwards, and the refusal window is asserted op by op; the in-process block proves the narrowed scope leaves a process the agent does not own running under the same uid; the fake-API block proves the patch is ordered after the quiesce reply, that a refused quiesce records no patch at all, and that an image without the feature is never even asked. A quiesce ending a command in flight and an open terminal is asserted through each one's own path (the `exec` resolves with its signal, the terminal receives its exit), and the single-flight rule is asserted from both sides. **Stays unproven here:** the general scan does not run at all on a host that forbids unprivileged user namespaces (those cases skip), and nothing was measured under Kata, whose guest kernel is the microVM's rather than the node's. `.changeset/workspace-quiesce.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sandbox` — a new opt-in op and method; `suspend()` and `destroy()` without the option send exactly the requests they sent before.

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#a-handle-follows-a-replaced-pod-and-says-so): a `KubernetesWorkspace` handle now reports what it is bound to (`identity`: `sandboxUid`, `volumeClaimUids`, `podUid`, `guestBootId`), follows a pod the controller replaced underneath it, and announces every guest change through `onGuestRestart` (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/identity.ts`, `workspace.ts`, `transport.ts`, `packages/sandbox/agent/agent.cjs`, `packages/sandbox/k8s/manifests/rbac.yaml`). A handle only worked while the pod it first bound to was alive: the agent's bind token is that pod's `metadata.uid`, read once per session, and under the DEFAULT `agentAddress: 'service'` the Service FQDN outlives the pod and keeps resolving — so after an eviction, a drain or a foreign `suspend()`/`resume()` the dial SUCCEEDED against the replacement and the guest refused every call, in two unrelated shapes (a named error for `exec` and `listFiles`, a bare `Error('unauthorized')` for `writeFile`, `readFile`, `openTerminal` and `openTcpConnection`). #472's rebind could not help by design — its own comment says only a dial failure reaches it. A refused call now runs the SAME re-read routine, for both triggers and both address modes: `Running` with the same `sandboxUid` and a live pod with a different uid rebinds and retries **once** (safe because the guest checks the token before dispatch, so a refused request ran nothing); the same pod uid leaves the original error standing; a different `sandboxUid`, or no `Sandbox` at all, raises `KubernetesWorkspaceReplacedError` and **never** rebinds, because the workspace name is deterministic and the disk behind a new object is empty. Concurrent refused calls share one re-read, and the re-read never takes the transition lock, so a refusal inside a resume's own privilege probe cannot deadlock it. Every `onGuestRestart` payload names both pods, that path included: the identity is built from the uids the announcing routine is holding rather than read back off a handle whose transition has not finished, so the one event a host is most likely to act on — a pod somebody else replaced between a resume's bind and its probe — reports which pod died and which one the handle is on now, and `identity` read from inside the listener answers the same way. The page also documents the half no API read can see: a container the kubelet restarts in place keeps its pod, its uid and its token while every guest process dies, so the agent now stamps an optional `guestBootId` on the replies it was already sending (`reserve-execution`, `cancel-execution` including `unknown_execution`, `read-file`, `write-file`, and the terminal and TCP `ready` frames), advertised as `guest-boot-id` in `healthz` features with **no** protocol-version bump — an older guest reports nothing and a host must read that as "cannot tell me", never as a change. Every operation that cannot be rebound now fails with one named class instead of two. The unconfirmed-cancellation section gains the diagnosis rather than a new write: `onCancellationUnconfirmed` carries `guest`, `previous` and `current` beside `agent`, and an unconfirmed cancel whose guest is demonstrably gone rejects with `KubernetesWorkspaceGuestGoneError` (a subclass of `RemoteCancellationUnknownError`, so existing catchers are unaffected) — nothing is patched, the workspace stays `Running` and `suspended` stays `false`. The identity it carries is per COMMAND: `previous` is the pod that command's `reserve-execution` was accepted by and the agent process inside it, never whatever the handle is bound to by the time the diagnosis runs, so neither a restart the handle survived earlier nor a pod another call has already rebound to is blamed on it; the pod read decides the verdict and the boot id only settles what it leaves open; and `current` names an agent process only when that process belongs to the pod it names, and a workspace somebody else suspended is still named by `KubernetesWorkspaceSuspendedError` rather than by the guest-gone class, so the handle adopts the suspension and `resume()` works. The RBAC section and `packages/sandbox/k8s/README.md` add `get` on `persistentvolumeclaims`, needed only for `volumeClaimUids`; a Role without it leaves that map empty and changes nothing else.
- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#bounded-search-and-the-glob-and-grep-builtins): `walkFiles` is implemented on Kubernetes task sandboxes and on `KubernetesWorkspace` (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/sandbox.ts`, `workspace.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/testing/sandbox-conformance.ts`). The method table on that page previously listed it **Omitted** ("not in this batch"), which cost a host more than one method: the SDK's `glob` and `grep` builtins refuse any sandbox without `walkFiles` and both are in the default builtin set, so a host that registered them and moved from the Firecracker or docker backend to Kubernetes lost both tools with no change on its own side. The implementation adds no wire op and no guest change — it is the SDK's own `walkFilesViaExec` running the SDK's walk program as `node -e` through the existing `execute`, the same enumerator Firecracker and docker use, and the shipped image (`node:22-bookworm-slim`, whose agent is itself node) already had everything it needs. The page now describes what follows from the walk being an execution: it counts as busy for the whole walk rather than per entry, `options.signal` and the consumer's own `iterator.return()` both abort that execution and kill the walk's process group in the guest, a cancellation the guest cannot confirm retires the sandbox exactly as a failed `exec` cancel does (no second rule for walks), and an exhausted examined-entry budget raises `ERR_FILE_WALK_LIMIT` rather than a short list. On a workspace it passes the synchronous `admit()` gate once, when the consumer asks for the first entry, so a suspended workspace refuses a walk exactly as it refuses `readFile` (same class, same `noticedBy: 'admission'`, this operation's own name) and nothing is dialed; admission is not re-checked per entry, so a suspend landing mid-walk fails the walk the way the transport failed and the handle names it a suspension on its next data-plane call, where `admitted()`'s one-shot diagnosis lives. The [conformance section](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#running-the-conformance-suite) records `SANDBOX_CONTRACT_VERSION` moving to `3` for three added sections — `walkFiles`, several `exec` calls at once on one sandbox with no cross-talk between their results, and an `exec` whose `timeout` produces a timed-out result and really terminates the command — and states why the bump strands no deployed guest image: `walkFiles` is optional on `Sandbox` and skips where a backend omits it, and `timeout`/`timedOut` have been on the shared contract and in `agent.cjs` since protocol v2. [File discovery](sdk/file-discovery.md)'s list of adapters built on `walkFilesViaExec` now names Kubernetes beside Docker, ACI and Firecracker. New tests: `backends/kubernetes/__tests__/walk-files.test.ts` (real agent over loopback, real filesystem, including a 12,000-file fixture where breaking out after five entries is proved to leave no walk process in the guest via `/proc`, and a comparison of the `glob`/`grep` builtins against the local provider over the same tree) and `workspace-walk-files.test.ts`. `.changeset/kubernetes-walk-files.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sandbox` — an optional method a backend did not implement is now implemented, and the conformance suite a backend author runs gained cases.
- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#the-lease): a failed lease renewal now retries on a short capped backoff (one second, doubling, capped at whichever is smaller of thirty seconds or a twentieth of the TTL) instead of waiting a full half-TTL for the next attempt (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/lease.ts`). The old cadence made a single failed renewal a coin flip against the object's own `shutdownTime` — the retry and the expiry both landed roughly a TTL after the last success — so one API blip at the wrong moment could delete a live sandbox's pod mid-command. A success still resets the loop to the normal half-TTL cadence; a renewal that finds the object already gone (404/410) still stops the loop immediately. No public API changed.
- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#each-shipped-templates-securitycontext): the de-privileged guest agent and every `execute`/terminal child it starts now get a writable `HOME` (`packages/sandbox/k8s/entrypoint.sh`, `k8s/Dockerfile`, `k8s/scripts/capability-check.mjs`). `setpriv --reuid=/--regid=` (the exec both branches end with) changes only the running process's credentials, never the environment, so `HOME` previously stayed `/root` on the root path — a directory #469 already verified the agent uid cannot use, which made LibreOffice without `-env:UserInstallation`, `pip install --user`, npm's cache, and the fontconfig/matplotlib caches all fail against it. `entrypoint.sh` now resolves a writable home once, before EITHER of its two exec sites (the non-root branch and the root branch, both fixed together — a fix to only one would have left the other broken), and exports `HOME`, `USER`, `LOGNAME`, `XDG_CACHE_HOME` and `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` alongside the existing `NAMZU_SANDBOX_WORKSPACE`; `agent.cjs`'s pre-existing, unchanged `childEnvironment` is what actually carries the value into every child, since none of the five is prefixed `NAMZU_AGENT_`/`NAMZU_SANDBOX_`. Resolution order: `getent passwd "$AGENT_UID"` field 6 (the shipped image's own `useradd --create-home` already creates `/home/namzu` owned by the agent uid, so this is the common case) — a missing `getent`, no passwd entry, an entry under `$NAMZU_WORKSPACE_ROOT`, or a directory this uid still cannot be made to own is a fallback trigger, never a failure — then `/tmp/namzu-home-$AGENT_UID` (mode `0700`, owned by the agent uid, never under the workspace root, so it can never appear in `listFiles`/`walkFiles` or an archive). Usability is decided by adopting the directory (create-if-missing, `chown`, then read the result back) rather than by POSIX `-w`, which succeeds vacuously for root on a directory the agent uid cannot write to — precisely the bug this fixes. Only if both the passwd-resolved directory and the `/tmp` fallback are unusable does the pod refuse to start. `k8s/scripts/capability-check.mjs` now also prints `$HOME`, whether it exists, and whether a probe write succeeded, informationally (never failing the check itself), in the same style as its existing `Seccomp` and set-id lines. `k8s/__tests__/entrypoint.test.ts` gains `getent`/`stat` fakes and a `HOME for the guest agent and its children` suite (genuinely-absent `getent`, an unresolvable-then-adopted passwd home, an unowned passwd home falling back, the workspace-root exclusion, and root/non-root parity), plus a `k8s/__tests__/dockerfile.test.ts` check that `useradd` carries `--create-home`; `packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/__tests__/agent-home-propagation.test.ts` drives the real agent over a loopback socket to prove an unwritable `HOME` fails an `execute` child's own `touch` exactly as before this fix, and a writable one lets both an `execute` and a `terminal` child succeed and report it back — the propagation mechanism itself, which this change does not touch. **Stays unproven here** (no Kata cluster in this environment): whether Kata's guest runtime already populates `HOME` from `/etc/passwd` unaided on the task template's path, and the two application-level acceptance criteria (LibreOffice, `pip install --user`) — neither ships in this image. `.changeset/guest-home.md`, **major** for `@namzu/sandbox` — every task and workspace pod's exported environment now differs from before on the normal (non-error) path, not only in a misconfiguration; a derived image that relied on `HOME=/root` (the only thing running as root before this fix could have depended on) must set `HOME` itself after its own `FROM`.
- **Update** [The composer footer](cli/terminal-design.md#the-composer-footer): the `· orchestrate` marker now holds the permission-mode badge's own survival priority under width pressure, instead of being bundled with `· effort <level>` as one droppable unit (`packages/cli/src/tui/StatusBar.tsx`). A real-PTY pass (`research/conversation-evidence/tui-orchestrate-mode-results.md`) found that at 40 columns, with orchestrate mode on and no effort menu open, the footer showed no trace of it at all — `fitStatusLine` dropped the combined `effort`/`orchestrate` unit right after the working directory, well before the model, so an operator in a narrow pane had no way to see that this persistent, behavior-changing session setting was active. `orchestrate` is now its own segment in `StatusLineLayout`, dropped only after the working directory, the effort label, the cycle-key reminder and the model are already gone, and only as a last resort — whole, never truncated to a fragment of the word, and never at the cost of shrinking the badge itself to make room for it: below the width where `orchestrate` fits beside an already-fitted badge, the badge wins instead. The 100-column line is unaffected. `composer-footer-shows-the-active-mode.test.tsx` gains coverage at 100, 60 and 40 columns, on and off, with and without effort set, and with a long working directory; the file's own drop-order comment is updated to match. Re-verified against the real interactive TUI at 40x30 and 100x30 (`tui-orchestrate-mode-cli.mjs`, `tui-orchestrate-mode-drive.py`), appended to the existing results file rather than rewritten. `.changeset/orchestrate-footer-narrow-width.md`, **patch** for `@namzu/cli` — no prop or export changed shape, only the footer's own width-pressure behavior.
- **Update** Let a [Kubernetes workspace command outlive the connection watching it](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#a-command-can-outlive-the-connection-watching-it), and make the guest's command-timeout ceiling an operator's knob (`packages/sandbox/agent/agent.cjs`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/{transport.ts,workspace.ts}`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/firecracker/{protocol.ts,transport.ts}`, `packages/sandbox/k8s/manifests/sandboxtemplate-workspace.yaml`). A command's output and result were tied to the one host connection that started it: any socket reset made the controller cancel the command, and a cancel it could not confirm in eight seconds retired the pod — so a network blip could cost a workspace every open terminal. If the host process exited instead, nothing cancelled, the command ran on, and its output had been written only to a closed socket and kept nowhere; the only op that returned the record terminated a running command to give it to you. The guest now has ONE retained-output primitive, `OutputLog` — an ordered, size-bounded log of stdout and stderr in a single monotonically increasing byte-offset space, bounded by bytes rather than chunks, whose eviction advances the log's start offset so a read from before it is answered with a `droppedBytes` count — and three additive wire changes read it: `reserve-execution` accepts a caller-chosen `executionId` (reserving one the guest still holds reports its state instead of minting a second reservation, so a retried start runs the command once), `execute` accepts `retainOutput`, and the new `attach-execution` op replays from an offset, follows the live command and ends with one terminal frame. Attach is an observer and nothing else: it never signals the process, closing an attach connection is a no-op, and ending a command stays `cancel-execution`'s job. On the handle, `exec(command, argv, { executionId, detach, detachSignal, reattachWindowMs, onGap })` reattaches from the last offset it received when its connection fails — bounding only getting BACK, and disarming that bound once an attach succeeds — and, if it cannot, rejects with the new `KubernetesExecutionDetachedError` carrying the id and the byte offset to resume from. **It never cancels to reconcile**, so a lost connection now writes nothing to the object at all. New beside it: `attachExecution(executionId, { fromOffset, onOutput, onGap, signal })`, which resolves to the same `SandboxExecResult` and detaches rather than cancels when its signal aborts, and `cancelExecution(executionId)`, the confirmed-cancel path from any process holding the id, which retires nothing when it cannot confirm. A reported gap sets BOTH truncation flags on the result, because the retained log is one interleaved space and the loss cannot be attributed to either stream. The guest advertises `execution-attach` in `healthz` and a host asking for detach against an image without it is refused before the command is admitted — the wire protocol version is deliberately unchanged, as `write-file-parts` left it. Idempotency ends at the retention window and at pod replacement, and the page says so. The guest's 30-minute `MAX_TIMEOUT_MS` was a hard constant while every ownership limit beside it was an environment variable; it now reads `NAMZU_SANDBOX_MAX_TIMEOUT_MS` — the same variable `worker/server.js` has always read for the same limit — defaults to the unchanged 30 minutes, and names the variable in its refusal. Four variables join the workspace template's `env` block at their defaults: that one plus `NAMZU_AGENT_EXECUTION_LOG_BYTES` (1 MiB), `NAMZU_AGENT_MAX_RETAINED_OUTPUT_LOGS` (32) and `NAMZU_AGENT_EXECUTION_RETAINED_TTL_MS` (10 min), which together bound what retention can cost the 512Mi container. `packages/sandbox/README.md`'s protocol section documents the primitive and the ceiling. `SandboxExecOptions` is **untouched** and nothing here reaches the SDK's `Sandbox`, so `@namzu/sdk` needs no changeset. `.changeset/exec-detach-and-reattach.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sandbox` — every new capability is opt-in, the default exec path is byte-identical on the wire, and the timeout ceiling defaults to the value it has always had.
- **Update** Document the two bounds the [Kubernetes backend](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#the-two-bounds-this-backend-sets-itself) now sets for itself (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/k8s-client.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/firecracker/transport.ts`, `packages/sandbox/agent/agent.cjs`). `apiRequestTimeoutMs` (default `30000`, minimum `1000`, no disabling value) bounds every API request inside `createKubernetesClient`, covering `getToken()`, the connection and the body on both transports, and expiry raises the new `KubernetesApiTimeoutError` rather than the generic `failed:` error — the fix is in the client rather than at each call site because several of these requests are single-flight promises that run under the first caller's signal, so one signal-less `destroy()` against an unanswering API server used to pin every later caller. `streamHeartbeatMs` (default `15000`, `0` off) adds a per-stream, negotiated liveness heartbeat to `openTerminal` and `openTcpConnection`: three missed intervals resolve `exited`/`closed` on the host and run the guest's close cleanup, an agent that predates the field echoes nothing and nothing arms, both sides count bytes rather than whole frames so a large frame still arriving is proof of life, the echoed interval is honoured only between `100` ms and four times what was asked because each side does its own watchdog arithmetic with it, backpressure is not counted as silence, and `VsockTransportOptions.heartbeatMs` stays undefined so the Firecracker tier's streams are unchanged. The config table gains both rows; `packages/sandbox/README.md`'s guest-transport section gains the wire's `stream-heartbeat` feature string.
- **Update** Stop [Kubernetes workspace failure paths](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#nothing-but-an-explicit-delete-deletes) from suspending a workspace that is in use (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/workspace.ts`, `sandbox.ts`, `transport.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/remote-execution-controller.ts`). Two paths sent an `operatingMode: Suspended` patch that no caller asked for, and that patch makes the controller delete the pod — every terminal, dev server and running command in it, for every holder. (1) A failed create, adopt or resume patched `Suspended` on **any** error, and the errors that reach it include a caller's `signal` aborting during readiness, one 5xx or 429 on a Sandbox or pod GET (the client does not retry) and a privilege probe that overran its deadline — all of which happen to a SECOND process while the first is using the workspace, which is the normal case given that a workspace id is a name and not a lock. A start failure now patches only when this call moved the mode: it POSTed the object, or its `Running` patch took the object out of `Suspended`. `resume()` reads `spec.operatingMode` before patching rather than patching blind, so a resume that finds another process has already woken the workspace sends nothing and binds the pod that is there. The new `onStartFailure` option (`'suspend-if-woken'` by default, `'leave'` to write nothing on any start failure) sits on both `KubernetesWorkspaceOptions` and `KubernetesWorkspaceTransitionOptions`. The inverse case is kept deliberately: a workspace this call woke and then failed to start is suspended again, or it would be left `Running` with a pod nobody is using. (2) An execution whose cancellation could not be confirmed retired the handle, which on a workspace was the same suspend patch — so eight seconds of network loss under one `exec()`, or a pod evicted under an in-flight command, took the pod away from every holder. It now writes nothing at all: the `exec()` still rejects with `RemoteCancellationUnknownError`, now carrying `retirement: { accepted: false, reason: 'workspace-kept' }` (`reason` is a new optional field on `SandboxRetirementObservation`), the handle goes on admitting calls, and one bounded `healthz` over a fresh connection reports through the new `onCancellationUnconfirmed({ error, agent })` callback whether the agent is `'ok'`, has fenced itself (`'retiring'`) or could not be reached (`'unreachable'`) — a distinction the transport's boolean `healthz()` collapses. A host that wants the old behaviour calls `suspend()` from that callback. A reservation refused with `agent_retiring` on the Kubernetes adapter is now `KubernetesAgentRetiringError`, naming `suspend()` then `resume()`, instead of `RemoteProtocolError: remote sandbox returned an invalid execution reservation`; unlike the Firecracker tier's mapping of the same refusal it does not retire the handle. Nothing here adds a DELETE — `deleteDisk: true` and `deleteKubernetesWorkspace` remain the only paths that remove a disk. New on the public surface: `KubernetesWorkspaceStartFailurePolicy`, `KubernetesWorkspaceAgentState`, `KubernetesWorkspaceCancellationNotice`, `KubernetesAgentRetiringError`, `KubernetesWorkspaceOptions.onStartFailure`/`.onCancellationUnconfirmed`, `KubernetesWorkspaceTransitionOptions.onStartFailure` and `SandboxRetirementObservation.reason`. `.changeset/workspace-failure-paths-keep-the-pod.md`, **major** for `@namzu/sandbox` — two observable defaults moved, and the changeset names how to restore each.
- **Update** Read a large file out of a sandbox in bounded memory — [reading a file larger than one frame](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#reading-a-file-larger-than-one-frame) (`packages/sandbox/agent/agent.cjs`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/firecracker/{transport.ts,protocol.ts,index.ts}`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/{transport.ts,sandbox.ts,workspace.ts}`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/{docker,aci-standby-pool}/index.ts`, `packages/sdk/src/types/sandbox/index.ts`, `packages/sdk/src/sandbox/provider/local.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/testing/sandbox-conformance.ts`). `Sandbox.readFile` took no options and returned the whole file from one request and one reply frame, and the guest built that frame by loading the file, base64-encoding it into one JSON object and writing it — so the file buffer, the base64 string, the JSON string and two frame buffers all existed at once, about 7.7x the file, in the container the workload shares. Measured against the shipped agent over loopback TCP (`VmHWM` of the agent's OWN process; under the shipped image `tini` is PID 1, so `/proc/1` measures the init), a 64 MiB read grew it by **405 MiB** against the workspace template's `512Mi` limit, and a file of about 384 MiB or more could not be read at all — its base64 string is longer than V8 lets a string be. Reads now have two further shapes. `read-file` takes optional `offset`/`length` and `pread`s one slice, answering with the WHOLE file's `sizeBytes` beside it and with the bytes that exist when the range runs past the end; one slice is one frame, so above `NAMZU_AGENT_READ_FILE_RANGE_BYTES` (1 MiB) the guest REFUSES rather than shortening, because a caller that asked for 4 MiB, got 1 MiB and was told nothing would read the short answer as the end of its range. A new authenticated `read-file-stream` op opens the fd once and sends `meta`, then base64 `data` frames, then `end`, then the zero-length terminator — `execute`'s terminated-stream shape — reusing one read buffer and waiting for the socket to drain between chunks, so a 1 GiB read grows the agent by about **12 MiB** at the 256 KiB default chunk (`NAMZU_AGENT_READ_FILE_STREAM_CHUNK_BYTES`; 1 MiB chunks measured 74.5 MiB, above the 64 MiB budget, which is what chose the smaller default at roughly 40% more wall time per gigabyte). A whole-file `readFile(path)` is built on the stream against a guest that advertises the capability, so existing callers lose the ceiling with no code change — the bytes are copied into one allocation sized from the `meta` frame rather than collected and concatenated, since concatenating needs every chunk to exist at once and costs twice the file (2.3x measured on a 256 MiB read, against the 2x it is held to). The guest opts in: `agent.cjs` advertises `read-file-stream` in `healthz` beside `write-file-parts`, ONE string for both read shapes because they ship in the same file; against a guest that does not advertise it a whole-file read takes the unchanged single-frame path and a ranged read or a `readFileStream` throws the new exported `AgentReadFileStreamUnsupportedError` before dialing, because such an agent ignores `offset`/`length` and answers with the whole file, which the caller would read as its slice. The guest wire protocol version is **unchanged**. A regular file whose `stat` reports no size — the procfs/sysfs shape, reachable only through `NAMZU_SANDBOX_READ_ROOTS` — is read to EOF rather than answered as empty on BOTH new shapes: a whole-file read is now served by the stream and `fs.readFile` read those to EOF, and a ranged read reads the file once to get a true size, since every number a range is made of would otherwise come from the zero `stat` gave and the caller would be told the file is empty. A range must ask for `base64` (`read_file_range_requires_base64`), because a `utf8` slice at an arbitrary offset can split a multi-byte character, and the stream serves regular files only (`read_file_stream_not_a_regular_file`), so a whole-file read of a fifo or a device node — which only `NAMZU_SANDBOX_READ_ROOTS` can put within reach — is now refused rather than attempted, and a file that shrinks under the open fd fails the read instead of coming back short. Both new shapes go through the same `resolveReadablePath` + `realpathWithinWorkspace` jail, and on the Kubernetes backend `readFileStream` is rebound to a replaced pod only around its FIRST chunk — a re-dial after bytes have been yielded would restart the file and the consumer would concatenate a duplicate prefix. New on `@namzu/sdk`: `readFile`'s optional second parameter, the optional `readFileStream?` method and `SandboxReadFileOptions`; source-compatible in both directions, since a caller may omit the parameter and a backend may declare the narrower form. `defineSandboxConformance` gains a 9 MiB read and an explicit byte range, gated on the new `supportsRangedAndStreamedReads` option; those two cases deliberately did NOT raise `SANDBOX_CONTRACT_VERSION` any further (`walkFiles`, concurrent `exec` and the `exec` timeout took it from 2 to 3, and none of the three needs a guest feature that did not already exist), because the Firecracker tier's guest lives in a golden rootfs image that is not built from this repository — nothing here builds one and `packages/sandbox/package.json#files` does not ship `agent/` — so a contract bump would assert something about images this repository cannot see; the three suites that DO run this repository's agent set the flag true and every other backend gets the two cases as named, counted skips. `KubernetesWorkspace` forwards `readFile`'s options to the guest and gains `readFileStream`, both narrowed to present and both admitted like every other data-plane call, so a suspended workspace refuses them by name. The three backends that cannot serve a range now REFUSE one instead of ignoring it: the SDK's local provider serves it from one positional read, and the docker and standby-pool workers — whose HTTP `/read-file` has no range — throw, because a backend that takes `offset`/`length` and answers with everything is the silent wrong answer the contract exists to forbid; all three also honour `options.signal`, which the contract documents as aborting the read. `packages/sandbox/README.md`'s protocol section and `k8s/README.md`'s contract-suite step document the new shapes. `.changeset/read-file-ranged-and-streamed.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sandbox`; `.changeset/sandbox-read-file-options.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sdk`.
- **Update** Document the default-on ingress check on the [Kubernetes sandbox page](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#ingress) (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/ingress-policy.ts`). A new section names `k8s/manifests/networkpolicy.yaml` as required, states the union rule the check implements (one ingress-enforcing policy must select the pod, and no policy selecting it may admit a wide-open peer on the agent port), tabulates every source shape counted as wide open under core `NetworkPolicy` and under the Cilium CRD, and records what a namespaced `Role` cannot see and therefore why `ingress: 'unverified'` is a legitimate configuration rather than a smell. The RBAC section gains `list` on `networkpolicies` (and, under `engine: 'cilium'`, on `ciliumnetworkpolicies`) with the reason `list` rather than `get` is what a selector check needs; the configuration table gains `ingress` and the previously undocumented `egress`; the agent-credential section keeps calling the network the primary boundary and gains the paragraph saying it is now checked rather than assumed. The deployment section gains a sixth acceptance row, `k8s/scripts/ingress-check.mjs`, together with the statement that a cluster whose CNI does not enforce `NetworkPolicy` makes that script report FAIL by design. The refusal section documents `unread`, the collections the check could not enumerate: an empty `examined` list means either that the namespace holds no policy or that none was read, so the message says “the namespace holds no policy of the kinds read” only when a list actually came back empty, names granting the missing verb rather than applying a policy when it never got to look, and still reports whatever WAS read beside what was not — the sentence naming the fix says the read was partial rather than that nothing was read. ‘What it will not guess’ gains the wire-reading rule — a field arriving as something the schema does not declare (a `spec.ingress` that is not a list of rules, a `podSelector` that is not a selector, a `fromCIDR` that is a string) refuses rather than being read as its empty value — names `reserved:` as the Cilium label source the check cannot map, and records that a Cilium rule carrying `enableDefaultDeny: { ingress: false }` cannot count as coverage while what it admits is still read as an opening — and a rule there this check cannot read is undecidable, not something the policy that closes nothing gets to swallow; the wide-open table’s residual bullet now names `fromEndpoints: [{}]` beside core’s `podSelector: {}`. Three shipped statements that claimed coverage that did not exist are corrected in the same commit: `k8s/manifests/sandboxtemplate-task.yaml`, `k8s/manifests/networkpolicy.yaml` and the `egress` comment in `packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/index.ts`, and the workspace template's inline `networkPolicy` block is marked as covering no workspace pod.
- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#a-holder-epoch-fences-every-lifecycle-write): every Kubernetes workspace lifecycle write can now be conditioned on a monotonic holder epoch stored on the `Sandbox` itself (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/objects.ts`, `k8s-client.ts`, `workspace.ts`, `index.ts`). The page previously ended its adoption section with "a workspace id is a name, not a lock … that coordination is the caller's", which was true and unhelpful: a host that keeps its own holder epoch still could not make the decision stick, because "check my epoch, then call `suspend()`" is check-then-act and the write that follows is a separate request the API server accepts — so a late `suspend()` stopped the new holder's pod, a late `destroy({ deleteDisk: true })` took the disk, and a late adopt woke a workspace that had just been suspended. `createKubernetesWorkspace`, `suspend()`, `resume()`, `destroy()`, `suspendKubernetesWorkspace` and `deleteKubernetesWorkspace` now accept `epoch`, a handle keeps the one it was opened or last resumed with and writes under it (including the cleanup patch a failed start sends), and a write carrying epoch `e` applies only when the stored epoch is `<= e`, storing `e` in the same request. The page documents the annotation (`sandbox.namzu.ai/holder-epoch`), the `~1`-escaped JSON Pointer, the `application/json-patch+json` dialect a conditional patch goes up as, the `preconditions.resourceVersion` a fenced `DELETE` carries, `KubernetesWorkspacePreconditionError` and the guarantee that a refusal changes nothing on the cluster **and** nothing on the handle — it is decided before `suspend()` reaps terminals. Two things are stated because a reader would otherwise think the page contradicts itself or the API: how an annotation condition plus a one-shot `resourceVersion` fallback squares with the backend's "no watch, no informers, no resourceVersion tracking" invariant, and what a real API server actually answers an unapplied JSON patch with — measured on v1.37.0 against the agent-sandbox `Sandbox` CRD, a failed `test` and a malformed body are the same opaque 422 `Invalid`, so the backend re-reads the object to tell a lost race from a wrong body rather than claiming to read it off the reply. Opting in is a decision: a call that passes no epoch sends the requests it always sent, merge-patch content type included, and `listKubernetesWorkspaces` reports each workspace's stored epoch as `holderEpoch` rather than conditioning a read nothing writes. No new RBAC — the `patch` verb covers every patch type. `packages/sandbox/README.md`'s workspace paragraph gained the same summary.
- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#a-terminal-or-a-program-can-outlive-the-host-process): a workspace terminal or background program can now outlive the host process that started it (`packages/sandbox/agent/agent.cjs`, `src/backends/firecracker/protocol.ts`, `transport.ts`, `src/backends/kubernetes/transport.ts`, `workspace.ts`).
