A terminal used to belong to the framed connection that opened it, so a deploy, a crash or an OOM kill tore down every terminal the host had open; replay was buffered in the host process, so its successor had neither the output nor a way to name the terminal; and nothing could run outside a terminal at all, `exec` being capped at thirty minutes and killing the process group when the cap fires. The guest now keeps a session registry keyed by a caller-chosen `sessionId`, and the page documents the new workspace surface against it: `openTerminal({ sessionId, persistent: true })`, `attachTerminal`, `startDetached`, `readSession`, `listSessions` and `killSession`, with `AgentSessionDetachedError` as the rejection a session terminal's `exited` carries when the ATTACHMENT ends and the program does not — resolving it would report an exit that never happened. It also records what the registry is not: it is the pod's memory and nothing else, so `listSessions()` is empty after a suspend and resume, and `spawnDetached` stays omitted because it hands back a host `ChildProcess` that cannot cross a process boundary, which is why the new verb has a different name and returns a name rather than a process. The page's method table now says so in both rows. **One behaviour change is not opt-in and is written up as such:** the page previously repeated the agent's own claim that a terminal teardown reaches "the shell and every descendant", and it did not — util-linux `script` starts the shell in a new session, so a process-group kill reached `script` alone and a job backgrounded with `&` kept running with no terminal, holding its port, reachable by no op, until the pod stopped. Both a `killSession` and a plain terminal's teardown now signal every process still in that kernel session, found through `/proc`; a host that was relying on the leak moves that program to `startDetached`. Three knobs join the shipped workspace template's `env` block at their defaults (`NAMZU_AGENT_MAX_SESSIONS`, `NAMZU_AGENT_SESSION_LOG_BYTES`, `NAMZU_AGENT_SESSION_TERMINAL_TTL_MS`), and the page states that a session's ring is the same `OutputLog` a detached execution uses rather than a second retained-output registry — one overflow accounting, one offset space, one way to ask what was lost. The page also states the two rules that keep the surface honest under a reader: a `readSession` is a read and nothing more — it displaces no live attachment and signals nothing, so polling a shell's tail cannot end the terminal doing the polling — and a `kill` frame is coerced to the terminal signal set (`SIGTERM`, `SIGKILL`, `SIGINT`, `SIGHUP`) in ONE place, so it means the same thing on the connection that opened a terminal and on a later attachment to it. The guest advertises `sessions` in its `healthz` features and every session verb is refused by name against an image without it, never downgraded to a connection-bound terminal; the guest wire protocol version is unchanged.
- **Update** Give the [Kubernetes egress translation](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#egress) a word for "no network" and for "the public internet only", and verify EVERY policy that selects the sandbox pods rather than one object by name (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/egress-policy.ts`, `ingress-policy.ts`, `index.ts`, `workspace.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/index.ts`, `k8s/manifests/{rbac,networkpolicy,sandboxtemplate-task}.yaml`, `k8s/scripts/egress-check.mjs`). Three defects, one change. (1) `deny-all` was never "no network": it emits UDP/TCP 53 to `kube-system` and a cluster resolver forwards outside names upstream, so a sandbox configured to have no network kept a channel out — and because verification of the named object is an exact match, an operator could not remove that rule locally without failing every create. `{ kind: 'no-network' }` is therefore a NEW kind (`policyTypes: ['Egress']`, `egress: []`, no resolver at all — the agent needs none, the host dials in), and **`deny-all` and `allow-all` emit byte-for-byte what they always have**, pinned by deep equality before anything else in their test file is asserted. (2) `allow-all` reaches the node, the API server, the service network, the instance-metadata address and every other sandbox pod, and nothing meant "out but not sideways": `{ kind: 'public-internet', exceptCidrs? }` emits DNS to the resolver's own pods plus `0.0.0.0/0` except RFC 1918, `100.64.0.0/10`, `169.254.0.0/16`, `127.0.0.0/8` and `168.63.129.16/32`, and `::/0` except `fc00::/7`, `fe80::/10` and `::1/128`; a deployment's own `exceptCidrs` are added to that list, routed to the block of their own address family, and an entry that is not a CIDR is refused at construction with the new `KubernetesEgressPolicyConfigError`. Both kinds live on `KubernetesEgressConfig.policy` only — the tier-wide `EgressPolicy` union is untouched, so `@namzu/sdk` needs no changeset. (3) Verification read ONE object while the API server UNIONS every policy selecting a pod, and memoized the pass for the backend's lifetime: any second policy silently widened egress, including the controller-managed one a `SandboxTemplate`'s own `networkPolicy` block becomes — which in agent-sandbox v1.0.2 REPLACES the controller default rather than layering under it, so dropping the block opens the internet while the named object still verifies. Setting `config.egress` now also LISTs the namespace's `NetworkPolicy` objects (and, under `engine: 'cilium'`, that CNI's CRD), evaluates each selector against the pod's REAL labels, and refuses with `KubernetesEgressPolicyUnionError` when any selecting policy allows what the translation does not (`policy-widens-egress`; under `no-network`, any egress rule at all), when nothing selecting the pod puts it in egress default-deny (`no-enforcing-policy`, skipped under `allow-all`), or when a policy, peer, port or collection cannot be read (`not-evaluable`). The refusal names the pod's labels and every policy examined with a verdict each. It reuses `ingress-policy.ts`'s own enumeration and shape-reading helpers — `listPolicies`, `matchesLabelSelector`, `corePeerIsWideOpen`, the `ciliumSelectorKey`/`ciliumIdentityLabels` pair — rather than shipping a second evaluator, and `UnreadIngressPolicySource` is renamed `UnreadPolicySource` (the old name kept as a `@deprecated` alias) because both directions now report it. A pass is cached per label set for at most five minutes rather than for the backend's lifetime, and a failure is never cached; no watch, no informer and no `resourceVersion` is involved. It runs where the ingress check runs — before the POST for a directly created Sandbox, where the labels are knowable and a refusal leaves nothing behind, and after the bind for a claimed one, where a refusal releases the claim through the acquire path's existing cleanup — and on `createKubernetesWorkspace` before any resume patch. `egress.verify: 'named-object-only'` restores the previous single-object check exactly, and a test proves it issues that one GET and no enumeration. `rbac.yaml` already grants `list` on both policy resources (the ingress check's, landed the same day); the comments there now say the egress union check needs it too, and a manifests test asserts the verb rather than leaving it to the comment. The task template's claim that the managed policy "still applies underneath" is corrected, and `networkpolicy.yaml`'s note that the two objects "compose rather than conflict" now says what that composition costs. **Stays unproven here**: enforcement itself. `kind` accepts every `NetworkPolicy` and enforces none, and runs no Cilium data plane, so every "it was blocked" probe there passes for the wrong reason; the new `k8s/scripts/egress-check.mjs` is the live check and runs two positive controls (a loopback TCP dial that must be open, a `localhost` resolution that must succeed) before it reads any real probe, reporting FAIL on a non-enforcing cluster by design. The `static`/`resolver` hostname allowlist is enforced at L7 by that CNI's agent and is probed by nothing in this repo. `.changeset/egress-kinds-and-union-verification.md`, **major** for `@namzu/sandbox` — a deployment with any second policy selecting the sandbox pods now fails `create()` where it used to succeed.
- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#a-crashed-hosts-claims-labels-release-and-capacity): a host process can now label its own task-path claims, recover a crashed predecessor's by that label well before `claimTtlSeconds` reaps them, and read warm-pool headroom before admitting more work (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/{index,objects}.ts`, `src/index.ts`, `k8s/manifests/rbac.yaml`). A `SandboxClaim`'s own name is client-generated per acquire, so nothing about one said which host process created it — a host killed by a deploy, an OOM or a lost node left every claim it held running until the TTL backstop (an hour by default), and its replacement had no way to find, let alone release, them sooner. Three additions, all opt-in: `claimLabels?: Record<string, string>` on the backend config is written into `SandboxClaim.metadata.labels` only, never into `additionalPodMetadata` (a claim's own labels and a Sandbox's pod labels stay two different objects); `releaseKubernetesTaskSandboxes(config, { labelSelector, signal })` LISTs claims by that selector and DELETEs each one, returning `{ deleted, names }` — `labelSelector` is required and refused, before a single request goes out, if it is absent or empty, since a fallback to matching every claim would delete a live fleet's work; `readKubernetesTaskCapacity(config, { signal })` is three GETs and no writes — the named `SandboxWarmPool`, the claims collection filtered to `spec.warmPoolRef` matching that pool, and the pods collection counted by `Pending` phase — into `{ warmPool: { ready, desired }, activeClaims, pendingPods }`, and requires `warmPoolName` since a pool-less backend has no pool to report on. Both are standalone functions in the shape `listKubernetesWorkspaces`/`deleteKubernetesWorkspace`/`suspendKubernetesWorkspace` established, exported from `src/index.ts` beside them. `k8s/manifests/rbac.yaml` gains `list` on `sandboxclaims` (the crash-recovery verb both functions need — a deployment that does not re-apply it gets a `403` from either function alone, never from `create()`) and re-comments the pre-existing `sandboxwarmpools: get` as a backend need rather than only `acquire-p50.mjs`'s own diagnostic. A host setting no `claimLabels` sends the exact claim body it always has. New tests: `backends/kubernetes/__tests__/claim-labels-and-capacity.test.ts` (the labelled and unlabelled claim bodies against a real acquire, the empty-selector refusal, LIST-then-DELETE-exactly-those-names, an already-gone claim still counting as released, the three-read mapping, and a burst of many claims and pods asserted against `server.requests`); `k8s/__tests__/manifests.test.ts`'s RBAC verb-set assertion is updated. **Stays unproven here**: the real agent-sandbox controller's own claim-to-pool binding counts and its garbage collection on a claim `DELETE`, and the SIGKILL-a-host/release-from-a-second-process scenario end to end — this environment's kind cluster answers the API server directly but has no route to a bound pod, so the acquire path's privilege probe cannot complete from here (the standing "no live pod-ip validation from this machine" limit); `readKubernetesTaskCapacity` and `releaseKubernetesTaskSandboxes` were exercised against the real API server and the real controller with claims created directly at the claim-body shape `buildClaimBody` sends, not through a full `provider.create()`. `.changeset/claim-labels-release-and-capacity.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sandbox` — additive config plus two new standalone functions, no default changed.
- **Update** Let a [Kubernetes `static`/`resolver` egress allowlist](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#narrowing-a-staticresolver-allowlist-ports-dns-names-tls-server-names) be narrowed by port, DNS name and TLS server name (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/egress-policy.ts`, `index.ts`, `workspace.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/index.ts`, `k8s/manifests/{networkpolicy,sandboxtemplate-task,sandboxtemplate-workspace}.yaml`, `k8s/README.md`, `k8s/scripts/egress-check.mjs`). Unnarrowed, a `static`/`resolver` allowlist under `engine: 'cilium'` allows an ADDRESS rather than a hostname (any site sharing an allowlisted host's CDN address), ANY port on it, and ANY name to resolve at all (`rules.dns: [{ matchPattern: '*' }]`) — measured on AKS with Cilium 1.18.11: `curl --resolve example.com:443:<registry.npmjs.org's own address>` returned the wrong site's content, and `github.com:22` accepted a connection. `KubernetesEgressConfig.ciliumNarrowing` (`ports`, `hostPorts`, `dnsNames`, `tlsServerNames`, `tlsPorts`) closes each independently, valid only under `engine: 'cilium'` with a `static`/`resolver` policy — set anywhere else it throws the new `KubernetesEgressNarrowingUnsupportedError` synchronously, both from `buildKubernetesBackend` and `createKubernetesWorkspace`, before any request. **With every field unset the translation is byte-for-byte what it always emitted**, pinned by deep equality, so an already-applied policy keeps verifying after the upgrade — the whole point, since `verifyEgressPolicyApplied` is an exact match. Setting any field switches from one `toFQDNs` rule naming every host to one rule PER host, so ports and server names can differ host by host: `ports`/`hostPorts` become `toPorts` (a global default plus a per-host override); `tlsServerNames` adds `serverNames: [<host>]` to the host's TLS ports (default `[443]`, override with `tlsPorts`) and, with no port list configured for that host, limits it to those TLS ports rather than leaving it open, since a `serverNames` rule needs a port to attach to; `dnsNames` replaces the DNS-visibility rule's `matchPattern: '*'` with an exact `matchName` per host plus the host under `<namespace>.svc.<clusterDomain>`, `svc.<clusterDomain>`, `<clusterDomain>` (`cluster.local` default) and any configured extra search suffixes — exact names because Cilium's `matchName` does not match across a `.`. Every new field becomes part of what `verifyEgressPolicyApplied` requires (no new comparison logic — it already deep-equals the whole `spec.egress`), and the union check that reads every OTHER policy selecting the pod now tracks ports per allowed hostname too (`EgressAllowance.fqdns` carries a port set per entry, read through the same `readCiliumRulePorts` helper the translation and the checker now share), so a second `CiliumNetworkPolicy` naming an allowed host on a wider port — or with no `toPorts` at all — is caught as `policy-widens-egress` even though the hostname itself is allowed. A port outside `1-65535`, an explicitly empty `ports`/`hostPorts[host]`/`tlsPorts` array, or an empty `clusterDomain`/search suffix is refused at construction with `KubernetesEgressPolicyConfigError`. **The shipped manifests needed an explicit edit, not only documentation, and that edit is now enforced rather than only requested**: `networkpolicy.yaml` and both `sandboxtemplate-*.yaml` templates' managed `networkPolicy` ship a plain, L4-only kube-dns rule selecting the same pods on the same port as the narrowed `CiliumNetworkPolicy`'s own DNS-visibility rule — and Cilium's own precedence rule says an L4-only rule cancels the L7 portion of a similar rule that carries one, so left in place it undoes DNS-name narrowing (every name resolves again) on a deployment that applied the shipped files as-is. Each file says, at the rule itself, to delete it when `ciliumNarrowing.dnsNames` is on, and — because reachability alone cannot tell that plain rule apart from a narrowed one, both reaching the identical peer and port — `EgressAllowance` now carries the exact names a narrowed translation restricts DNS lookups to (`dnsNarrowedTo`), and the union check reads a candidate rule's OWN DNS restriction (a plain `NetworkPolicy` has none; a second `CiliumNetworkPolicy` only passes if its own `rules.dns` names a subset of the same list) before accepting it, in both `coreEgressRuleVerdict` and `ciliumEgressRuleVerdict`. Under the default `egress.verify: 'union'`, leaving the shipped rule in place with DNS-name narrowing on now fails the next `create()` with `policy-widens-egress` naming that policy, rather than silently doing nothing; `egress.verify: 'named-object-only'` skips the check and still needs the rule deleted by hand. Port and TLS-server-name narrowing need no manifest change. **Stays unproven here, as it must**: real Cilium L7 enforcement of any of the three options — `kind` runs no Cilium data plane, so a "the port is closed" probe there would pass for the wrong reason; `egress-check.mjs` and its README section say plainly that the hostname allowlist, narrowed or not, is not probed by anything in this repository, and name the real-cluster evidence (a positive control plus a `cilium policy trace`/BPF policy dump) a reviewer should demand instead. `.changeset/cilium-egress-narrowing.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sandbox` — every field is opt-in and the default translation is unchanged.
- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#egress-profiles-one-warm-pool-several-network-modes): `config.egress` gains `profile` and `profileLabelKey`, so one `SandboxWarmPool` can serve several enforced network modes instead of needing a template, a pool and a policy per mode (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/egress-policy.ts`, `index.ts`, `workspace.ts`). A profile is a DNS-1123 label value that travels to three places from one composer — the `SandboxClaim`'s `spec.additionalPodMetadata.labels`, a directly created `Sandbox`'s (and a workspace's) pod template, and the translated policy's own selector — and the default policy name becomes `${sandboxTemplateName}-${profile}-egress`. The page now states the operator prerequisite first, because a profiled claim is refused without it: the agent-sandbox controller validates a claim's `additionalPodMetadata` against the `allowed-label-domains` key of the `agent-sandbox-config` ConfigMap in its own namespace, whose built-in default is `sandbox.users.io`, while this backend's default key is `sandbox.namzu.ai/egress-profile`. It also records what was measured rather than assumed: against agent-sandbox v1.0.2 on Kubernetes v1.37, six claims out of one two-replica pool alternating two profile values each adopted a replica that already existed, in 47-61 ms, with the controller patching the label onto the running pod and into the `Sandbox`'s own `spec.podTemplate`, and a key outside the allowlist refused with `Ready=False`/`InvalidMetadata` — which the acquire already fails fast on as `KubernetesAcquireError { reason: 'claim-rejected' }`, one taxonomy for a refused claim whether or not a profile is configured, now carrying the new `KubernetesPodLabelsRejectedError` as its `cause` so the refusal also names the pod labels this backend sent and the `egress.profileLabelKey` that moves the offending key. The acquire waits for the bound pod to actually carry every label this backend asked the controller for, on the existing readiness deadline and in the existing pod read, and refuses with `KubernetesPodLabelNotObservedError` rather than admitting an unlabelled pod, which would run under whatever policy does select it. A workspace is refused on the same principle one step earlier: adopting a standing `Sandbox` whose pod template does not carry the configured profile raises `KubernetesWorkspaceMismatchError` with the new `field: 'egressProfile'`, before any resume patch, because an ordinary adopt patches no pod template and the ingress and egress-union checks on that path run against the labels the POST would have stamped rather than against the object. The meeting with `refreshPodTemplate` is where a profile could have been lost silently, and is covered rather than left open: a refresh replaces `/spec/podTemplate` whole, so the composed pod labels now travel through `sandboxPodTemplate`, through `buildPodTemplateRefresh` and on the workspace HANDLE, and a refresh built from the `SandboxTemplate` alone would have patched the profile label off the object — leaving the replacement pod selected by no per-profile policy, with nothing on that path to read the label back, and a stamped revision no create would ever reproduce. Because the refresh writes the configured profile, the adopt refusal is lifted for a call that asks for one (and re-applied if the patch loses its `test`), which makes `refreshPodTemplate: true` the supported way to move an existing workspace between profiles; two cases in `workspace-template-refresh.test.ts` assert the patched pod template still carries the label, through a handle's `resume()` and through an adopt of a workspace that predates the profile. The 'claim is pristine' section is corrected in the same pass: its rule is about cold starts, and `additionalPodMetadata.labels` does not cost one, unlike `env` and `volumeClaimTemplates`. `k8s/scripts/egress-check.mjs` gains `--profile`/`--profile-label-key`, so the live check can be run once per profile against one pool rather than exercising the unprofiled policy. What no test here proves, and the page says so, is the egress DIFFERENCE between two profiles — that is enforcement, and kind runs no Cilium data plane and enforces no `NetworkPolicy`.

## 2026-09-16

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#each-shipped-templates-securitycontext): task sandbox pods run non-root with no capabilities, and the guest image carries no set-id binary (`packages/sandbox/k8s/manifests/sandboxtemplate-task.yaml`, `k8s/manifests/kind-overlay/patch-task-no-runtimeclass.yaml`, `patch-workspace-filesystem-pvc.yaml`, `k8s/Dockerfile`, `k8s/entrypoint.sh`). `sandboxtemplate-task.yaml`'s container previously set `securityContext.privileged: true` for a reason the template's own comment named as fictional — the pod sets no `NAMZU_WORKSPACE_DEVICE`, so `entrypoint.sh`'s format/mount branch never ran, and the image sets no `USER`, so any process bypassing the entrypoint (a `kubectl exec` shell, notably) ran as uid 0 with every capability the pod granted. `privileged` is now `runAsUser: 1001`, `runAsGroup: 1001`, `runAsNonRoot: true`, `allowPrivilegeEscalation: false`, `capabilities: { drop: [ALL] }` and `seccompProfile: { type: RuntimeDefault }` — the container-level shape of the Pod Security Standards `restricted` profile, with ids matching the Dockerfile's `AGENT_UID`/`AGENT_GID`. The kind overlay's `patch-task-no-runtimeclass.yaml` takes the same shape, and so does `patch-workspace-filesystem-pvc.yaml` — its PVC is `Filesystem`-mode with no device set either, so it behaves like a task pod at startup even though it patches the *workspace* template. `sandboxtemplate-workspace.yaml` itself keeps `privileged: true`, unchanged: its device branch genuinely needs `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` to `blkid`/`mkfs.ext4`/`mount` a raw block device before dropping every capability. `entrypoint.sh` now branches on its own `id -u` rather than assuming root: a non-root start refuses outright (naming the uid) if `NAMZU_WORKSPACE_DEVICE` is set, since a device cannot be formatted or mounted without `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, and otherwise execs `setpriv --no-new-privs -- tini -- node agent.cjs` directly — skipping the `--reuid`/`--regid`/`--clear-groups`/`--inh-caps`/`--bounding-set` flags a non-root process has no capability to run anyway — so `NoNewPrivs` is set without depending on the runtime honouring `allowPrivilegeEscalation: false`. The root path (`sandboxtemplate-workspace.yaml`'s pod) is unchanged. `k8s/Dockerfile` also strips every setuid/setgid bit its own packages carry (`RUN find / -xdev -perm /6000 -type f -exec chmod ug-s {} +`, after the package/user layers) — Debian's `util-linux`/`e2fsprogs` on `bookworm-slim` otherwise ship `su`, `mount`, `umount`, `passwd`, `chsh`, `chfn`, `gpasswd`, `newgrp`, `chage`, `expiry` and `/usr/sbin/unix_chkpwd` set-id, none of which this image's own processes use; a task image `FROM`ing this one and layering packages on top must repeat that exact step after its own installs. `../../src/backends/kubernetes/privilege-probe.ts` is unchanged — it still refuses any pod, root-started or not, that kept a capability a template forgot to drop. `k8s/scripts/capability-check.mjs` now also prints the guest's `Seccomp` value and its set-id file count, informationally (neither can fail the check — under a VM runtime a requested `seccompProfile` may still report `Seccomp: 0` inside the guest depending on the runtime's own configuration, which this page and the script's own comments now say explicitly). `k8s/__tests__/entrypoint.test.ts` shims `id` (defaulting to root, so every existing case is unaffected) and gains cases for the non-root branch; `k8s/__tests__/manifests.test.ts` asserts the task template's new `securityContext` shape and that the workspace template still runs `privileged: true`. `packages/sandbox/k8s/README.md` and this page document both shapes. `.changeset/sandbox-nonroot-task-pods.md`, **major** for `@namzu/sandbox` — a task pod's default runtime capabilities changed: a task workload that relied on root or an ambient Linux capability inside a task sandbox (mounting something, binding a privileged port, `CAP_NET_RAW`, etc.) now fails where it previously worked, and must either stop needing that capability or fork/override `sandboxtemplate-task.yaml`'s `securityContext` after applying it.

- **Update** [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#deployment): `k8s/entrypoint.sh` now refuses to format a workspace's block device on anything but `blkid`'s exit status 2 ("no filesystem found"), and only once a raw `dd` read confirms the device can actually be probed. Previously `2>/dev/null || true` folded every `blkid` failure — missing (127), not executable (126), a usage error (4), an ambivalent low-level result (8), or a bare 0 with no printed type — into "empty", so a `blkid` that could not run or could not answer looked identical to a genuinely blank disk and mkfs ran anyway; on an image whose `PATH` omits `blkid`'s directory (or ships a broken one) that formats a resumed workspace's already-populated disk. Every non-2, non-"0-with-a-type" status now aborts the pod instead, naming the status and leaving `blkid`'s own stderr on the container log rather than discarding it; a missing tool (`blkid`, `dd`, `mkfs.ext4`, `mount`, `chown`, or `setpriv`) is checked with `command -v` and named before anything runs. `k8s/__tests__/entrypoint.test.ts` gains cases for each failure mode (a `blkid` that exits 127/126/4/8, one entirely absent from `PATH`, and one that exits 2 against a device that then fails the readability probe), plus `fakes`/`basePath` options on its `runEntrypoint` harness so a case can prove a tool is truly unreachable rather than merely made to fail. `packages/sandbox/k8s/README.md` and this page describe the new behaviour. `.changeset/sandbox-blkid-exit-status.md`, **patch** for `@namzu/sandbox` — no public API changed; this is cluster-deployment guest-image/script behaviour, not published in the npm package.
- **Creation** Let the parent narrate the work it is coordinating, one bounded line at a time above the [agent rail](cli/terminal-design.md#the-composer-footer) (`packages/cli/src/integrations/subagents/activity.ts`, `runtime.ts`, `packages/cli/src/tui/AgentExplorer.tsx`, `App.tsx`, `agent.ts`). The rail says what each child is doing and nothing said why — which phase just returned, what disagreed, what happens next. A new `narrate_work` tool takes one `line` and `SubagentActivityMonitor` retains it beside the child rows: `narrate()` normalises it to a single row (control characters stripped, clipped at `MAX_NARRATION_CODE_UNITS` = 200 with the same `[clipped]` marker every other retained label uses), keeps at most `MAX_RETAINED_NARRATION` = 3 with the oldest dropped first, and clears on `reset()` and `close()` like every other buffer there. It answers with a `SubagentNarrationOutcome` — `shown`, `empty` or `closed` — rather than a line-or-nothing, because the two ways nothing is kept are different facts to whoever wrote the line: a blank line is the writer's to fix and a closed monitor is not, and the tool reports them as two different refusals. The tool's schema accepts up to twice the display width, so a sentence that overruns the row is clipped with the marker an operator can see while a paragraph is refused outright rather than reduced to its opening clause. `SubagentActivitySource` gains an optional `getNarration()` — optional because it is an additional projection over the same monitor, so a host that publishes child rows and no commentary stays a complete source — and `App` reads it in the same subscription that refreshes the rows. The new `AgentNarrationBand` (`AgentExplorer.tsx`) draws one dim row per line, unboxed, indented to the column the rail's own rows start in, between the composer footer and the rail's top border: the footer stays directly under the message frame, the band follows it, the rail follows the band. No agent row is traded for a line of commentary — `agentTaskPanelPageSize` computes the rail's budget from the terminal's own rows and never from what is above it — and on a screen with no row to spare the band's rows are paid for the way every row this TUI adds is paid for: the frame grows, the terminal scrolls, and the oldest conversation leaves at the top while the rail stays whole at the bottom. Measured in a real terminal 24 rows tall, with and without narration, by the new `research/conversation-evidence/narration-band-cli.mjs`; `tui-footer-order-render.mjs` is corrected in the same pass to read the emulator's VIEWPORT (`buffer.active.baseY`) rather than buffer row 0, which is the oldest line of scrollback once the screen has scrolled and made an intact rail read as one missing its last rows. It is not conditioned on the rail having rows — between two phases there are none, which is exactly when a line saying what comes next is worth the row — and it renders nothing at all when there is no commentary, so a run that never narrates is byte-identical on screen to before. Narration is the PARENT's voice by construction: the tool is registered on the parent conversation's registry beside `send_message` and `cancel_agent`, and a child's roster is the registry the host's own `buildTools()` builds, which carries none of them — a line a child wrote and this host rendered as the run's own would be untrusted text presented as trusted narration, the shape wrapping a child's output as untrusted exists to prevent, and a test holds the property at the roster rather than in a comment. Deliberately NOT a new `RunEvent`: that would spend public SDK surface and two exhaustive bridge mapper tables on a line of decoration the parent already knows. The tool is mounted only by a host with an operator watching (`AgentSessionOptions.askUser`, the condition `ask_user_question` already mounts under): `namzu run`, `run --stream`, `drain` and the resident step have no rail for a line to appear above, and a tool whose whole answer is "the operator saw this" must not be offered where there is no operator. It declares itself read-only and runs without review — it starts, changes and stops nothing of its own, and a consent dialog per line of commentary, shown to the operator being asked, is a tool nobody would call; `send_message` and `cancel_agent`, which reach into a running child, are reviewed exactly as before. A successful call adds no transcript row (`App.tsx`'s `tool-end` handling, in the branch beside the existing suppression of a represented `Agent` call and refused for the same stated reason: the band owns the line, so printing the call that wrote it would show one sentence twice, the second time as protocol; `NARRATION_TOOL_NAME` is exported from `runtime.ts` so the two surfaces cannot drift). A refused line keeps its ordinary row, since nothing reached the screen and that row is the only thing that says so, and the tool's `presentCall` labels that row `Narrating` rather than echoing the arguments. No status meaning, no percentage or fill bar. The band is in-memory only — nothing replays it onto the screen after a resume — while the CALL is recorded like every other tool call, so the line is in the run's transcript and checkpoints and comes back into the model's own history on `/resume`, which is a durability fact an operator and a model both act on, so the page states both halves rather than the flat claim it first made. [Delegated work](cli/delegated-work.md) documents the tool and its parent-only scope; [terminal design](cli/terminal-design.md#the-composer-footer) documents how the band is styled relative to the rail. `.changeset/parent-narration-band.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/cli` — a new operator-visible capability, nothing existing changed.

- **Creation** Add `/orchestrate`, a session mode layered above reasoning effort rather than inside it (`packages/cli/src/tui/slashCommands.ts`, `App.tsx`, `StatusBar.tsx`, `ChoicePicker.tsx`, `choice-selection.ts`; `packages/sdk/src/prompt/coding-agent-doctrine.ts`; `packages/cli/src/context/doctrine.ts`, `tui/agent.ts`). `/orchestrate [on|off]` (no argument toggles) turns the mode on or off; it is deliberately not a `ReasoningEffort` value — `/effort orchestrate` reports `unavailable for this model`, the same refusal `ultracode` gets today, and that existing guarantee and its test are unchanged. When the `/effort` picker can open, the mode also appears there as its own row below a horizontal rule (`ChoicePickerOption.ruleBefore`, new and generic, reserving one extra furniture row in `choicePickerWindowSize`) — a session setting shown apart from the levels above it, never a sixth level. Turning the mode on pins reasoning effort to the model's highest published level (the last entry of `reasoningEffortLevels`, since every provider publishes low-to-high) and sets `SendOptions.orchestrate` on future turns; when the model publishes no exact effort menu the mode still turns on and still sets that flag, but pins nothing and says so in the confirmation message. The mode is in-memory and per-session, like effort itself — no preferences file. A model switch still resets an explicit effort override to the new model's default, but while the mode is on it re-pins to the *new* session's highest level instead of clearing, and the interactive effort chooser that otherwise follows a standalone `/model` pick does not reopen on top of that automatic re-pin. The status line reads the level and the mode together (`effort high · orchestrate`), or the mode alone when nothing is pinned (`orchestrate`) — never `effort orchestrate`, which would misread as a fabricated level. On the SDK side, `codingAgentDoctrineContribution` gains an optional `orchestrate` flag and the new exported `CODING_AGENT_ORCHESTRATE_DOCTRINE` text, appended after the delegation doctrine only when the flag is on and delegation itself is not disabled; with the flag left at its default the rendered text is byte-identical to before this option existed, which the test suite pins directly. The CLI threads a turn-scoped `orchestrate` boolean through `AgentSession.send`'s `SendOptions` and includes that text in the per-turn system prompt only for a turn sent while the mode is on — the durable-resume prompt path is untouched, since the mode is not persisted and a resumed run has no live mode state to revive. Changesets: **@namzu/cli minor**, **@namzu/sdk minor**.
- **Update** Name unlabelled groups neutrally in the [agent cockpit's workflow picker](cli/terminal-design.md#the-composer-footer) too (`packages/cli/src/tui/AgentExplorer.tsx`). `agentWorkflows()` still groups an unlabelled batch by itself (two unrelated unlabelled batches stay two groups), but its `name` fell back to the literal `Delegated work` for any of them — the placeholder `SubagentActivityMonitor` gives every agent that never declared a `workflow`, so two such groups were indistinguishable rows in the picker even though the rail and cockpit header had already dropped that literal in the same-day rail/title fix above. An unlabelled group's name is now built from its own agents instead — the first member's title, plus a `+N` count once the group holds more than one, or a plain agent count when even a title is missing — never the generic default; a group with an explicit shared label is unaffected. `.changeset/footer-order-rail-title.md` gains one sentence covering this row; no new changeset, since it lands on the same unpublished minor.

- **Update** Move the [agent rail below the composer footer](cli/terminal-design.md#the-composer-footer) and title it by its actual workflow, not a fixed label (`packages/cli/src/tui/App.tsx`, `AgentExplorer.tsx`). Render order below the message frame used to be frame → delegated-work rail → footer; it is now frame → footer → rail (or, in its place, the agent cockpit, a child transcript, or the tool-output viewer — the same relative order those already had). The footer's own row is now defined relative to the message frame's bottom border rather than to the bottom of the viewport, since a live rail can now draw past it. `AgentTaskPanel`'s title and the agent cockpit's per-workflow header used to fall back to the literal string `Delegated work` — the same placeholder `SubagentActivityMonitor` gives an agent that never declared a `workflow` — whenever no single explicit label covered every shown agent; both now fall back to a neutral count instead (`2 agents · 1 running`), and still show the shared label when one covers every agent. `AgentExplorer.tsx`'s `COCKPIT_CHROME_ROWS` arithmetic is unchanged (the footer still occupies exactly one row outside the cockpit's own box, just above it now instead of below); its explanatory comment is updated to match. `.changeset/footer-order-rail-title.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/cli` — a visible layout change to the interactive TUI: the rail moves and its title text changes for any workflow that has not set an explicit label.
- **Update** Replace "The write-file size limit" with ["Writing a file larger than one frame"](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#writing-a-file-larger-than-one-frame) (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/firecracker/transport.ts`, `protocol.ts`, `packages/sandbox/agent/agent.cjs`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/transport.ts`, `sandbox.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/testing/sandbox-conformance.ts`). `Sandbox.writeFile` used to refuse a body above ~5.9 MiB on the kubernetes backend: every `tcp` request dials a fresh connection and the credential rides inside the envelope, so every request is that connection's first unauthenticated frame and is bounded by the guest's pre-auth ceiling (`NAMZU_AGENT_MAX_PREAUTH_FRAME_BYTES`, 8 MiB) on every call, and a `write-file` carries its whole base64 body in that frame. That page now documents the fix rather than the limit: a body that does not fit one frame is split into parts written to a temporary sibling of the target inside the same workspace jail, each naming the byte offset it starts at (the guest refuses a part whose offset is not the temp file's current size, so a lost, duplicated or reordered part is detected), finished by an atomic rename — so a reader never sees a half-written file, a failed or aborted write leaves the target untouched, and an abandoned sequence removes its temp file best-effort. The guest verifies what it actually wrote before renaming: one `pwrite` answers a write that crosses the volume's free space or an `RLIMIT_FSIZE` with a SHORT count and no error, so the count the syscall returned and the temp file's own size must both match what the part claimed or the part is refused (`write_part_short_write`) with nothing renamed — the final part is the exposure, an earlier one being caught by the next part's offset check. The page also names the one window a cancellation cannot close: a signal that fires after the final rename has landed but before its reply is read rejects a write that did happen. The guest opts in: `agent.cjs` advertises `features: ['write-file-parts']` in its `healthz` reply and the host chunks only for a guest that did, an old guest still getting the named `AgentPreauthFrameTooLargeError` (whose message now names the missing feature). The guest wire protocol version is **unchanged** — `part` is an optional field on an existing op. `part.discard`, the one verb on `write-file` that removes a file, is confined by name to the agent's own `.namzu-write-….part` files (`write_part_not_a_temp_file`, checked lexically as well as on the resolved path, and answering an idempotent success — without creating the directory it would have looked in — when there is nothing there), a `part` that is present but is not an object is refused (`write_part_invalid_shape`) rather than served as the plain whole-file write it resembles, and a final part's `renameTo` is resolved through the workspace jail BEFORE any byte is written, so a refused rename target leaves the temp file untouched. The page also gains the two new `VsockTransportOptions` knobs (`maxWriteFileBytes`, default 1 GiB, checked before the route is chosen so it bounds a single-frame write too, refused with the new `AgentWriteFileTooLargeError`; `writeFilePartBytes`, which the page now also names as the lever for a link slow enough that a part cannot clear the guest's pre-auth deadline), and records that `SANDBOX_CONTRACT_VERSION` moved to `2` because `defineSandboxConformance` gained a large-body case — which exercises the part path on the kubernetes (`tcp`) fixture and the single-frame path on the Firecracker (`unix`) one. `packages/sandbox/README.md`'s protocol section documents the `part` shape and the `features` field beside the pre-auth bounds table. `.changeset/write-large-files-in-parts.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sandbox` — additive options, additive exports, and a call that used to throw now succeeds.

- **Update** Give [Kubernetes workspaces](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#managing-workspaces-without-waking-them) a way to be listed, suspended and deleted without waking them, and let a handle notice a suspend another process performed (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/workspace.ts`, `objects.ts`, `k8s/manifests/rbac.yaml`). `createKubernetesWorkspace` adopts AND resumes and was the only entry point, so retention had no path — deleting a month-old suspended workspace meant starting its pod and probing it purely to tell it to go away — and neither did process hand-off. Three verbs now reach the object without opening it, creating no pod, dialing no agent and resuming nothing: `listKubernetesWorkspaces` (one GET of the sandboxes collection, returning `workspaceId`, `operatingMode`, `template`, `createdAt` and `operatingModeChangedAt` per workspace, sending no PATCH and no DELETE), `deleteKubernetesWorkspace` (one DELETE by the deterministic name, with `destroy({ deleteDisk: true })`'s own guarantees: already gone counts as deleted, a failure rejects and stays retryable) and `suspendKubernetesWorkspace` (the suspend patch plus the wait for the pod to actually stop). `operatingModeChangedAt` comes from a new annotation, `sandbox.namzu.ai/operating-mode-changed-at`, that the suspend and resume patches stamp — nothing already on the object answers the question, because upstream leaves the `Suspended` condition True across a resume, and a workspace whose mode never changed is reported without one rather than defaulted to `createdAt`. On the handle, [`refresh()`](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#a-handle-notices-a-suspend-it-did-not-perform) re-reads `spec.operatingMode` so a workspace somebody else suspended reports `suspended: true` and `resume()` brings it back on the same disk, and a call that fails while the handle believes it is running re-reads the object once and surfaces `KubernetesWorkspaceSuspendedError` — with the transport failure on `cause` and a new `noticedBy` field saying whether the handle knew before the call or found out from it — instead of the flat `unauthorized` that named nothing. A foreign suspend is recorded as unconfirmed: what was observed is the object's mode, not the pod stopping. New on the public surface: `listKubernetesWorkspaces`, `deleteKubernetesWorkspace`, `suspendKubernetesWorkspace`, `KubernetesWorkspaceSummary`, `KubernetesWorkspace.refresh()`, `KubernetesWorkspaceSuspensionNotice` and `KubernetesWorkspaceSuspendedError.noticedBy`. The host's Role gains `list` on `sandboxes`, the only new RBAC verb and `listKubernetesWorkspaces`'s alone — see the page's RBAC section.

- **Update** Let [adopting a Kubernetes workspace](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#calling-it-twice-reattaches) wait for the replacement pod when the previous one is still draining, and report how the handle was obtained (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/workspace.ts`). A create on an existing name adopts the standing object, and the second process to reach a workspace arrives at a moment the first did not choose: a `suspend()` that ended in `KubernetesWorkspaceSuspendTimeoutError` (the patch landed, the guest is riding out its `terminationGracePeriodSeconds`), two hosts coming up during a rollout, or a host restarting inside that same grace period. In all three the only pod under the Sandbox's name carries a `deletionTimestamp` — never bound to, because its uid is the agent's bind token and the pod's replacement refuses it — and the replacement does not exist yet, so the pod read threw and the adopt rethrew it instead of polling the way the resume path does. An adopt that finds the object `Suspended`, or finds its pod already terminating, now polls for a live pod under the same `readyTimeoutMs` budget everything else on the path uses, and the budget running out names the pod that was still terminating rather than reporting a generic missing uid. An adopt of an object that was Running with a healthy pod is unchanged and still fails on the first read, as the created path does: nothing is being replaced there, so waiting out the whole budget would only delay the answer the first read already gave. New on the public surface: `KubernetesWorkspace.origin` and the `KubernetesWorkspaceOrigin` union (`'created' | 'adopted-running' | 'resumed'`), both in `@namzu/sandbox`. It is fixed for the handle's life and answers what the call walked into rather than what state the workspace is in now, which is what `suspended` is for. A host adopting a workspace another process left running needs it: on `resumed` the pod is new and only the disk survived, and on `adopted-running` the pod is the same one and a detached command may still be running in it, but no terminal is — the guest agent kills a terminal's process group the moment its connection closes, and a dead host's connections closed with it. One more failure now reports what it was waiting for: a readiness budget that expires after the Sandbox has already been seen Ready is reported against the POD the wait was spending it on, instead of as "never became Ready" against a condition that has been True throughout.

- **Update** Give [Kubernetes sandboxes](sdk/kubernetes-sandbox.md#where-the-host-runs-decides-the-address) a choice of agent address, so a host outside the cluster can reach the guest agent (`packages/sandbox/src/backends/kubernetes/{index.ts,transport.ts,workspace.ts,objects.ts}`, `packages/sandbox/src/backends/firecracker/transport.ts`, `packages/sandbox/src/index.ts`). Every sandbox and workspace was dialed at `<name>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local`, which only cluster DNS resolves, so a host on a peered VNet with a routable pod range failed every call at name resolution — readiness and the privilege probe included, which made `create()` reject on its readiness budget naming a timeout. `KubernetesBackendConfig.agentAddress` now takes `'service'` (the default, and byte-for-byte the previous behaviour) or `'pod-ip'`, which dials the bound pod's IP read from the same `GET` that reads its uid, so the address and the bind token always come from one pod and never from the Sandbox's own `status.podIPs`. A pod is `Pending` with no `status.podIP` until the CNI attaches it — the window a resume deliberately binds inside of, since the Sandbox stays Ready across the transition — so under `'pod-ip'` an acquire and a resume both poll for the address on the readiness budget they already own and refuse only when it never arrives, naming the pod that never got one — and only an expired clock is reported that way, so an API failure or a deleted Sandbox during that wait still travels as itself; `'service'` still reads the pod exactly once. A pod IP dies with its pod, so a `resume()` re-reads it and a dial that fails at connect re-reads the live pod once: a new uid means the pod was replaced, and the handle follows the new address and token and retries, while an unchanged pod leaves the original error standing. A dial failure is classified by WHERE it came from — the dial's own wrapper, so nothing reached the guest — rather than by `errno`, since `ETIMEDOUT` is also what an established socket raises mid-request. A dial of a Service FQDN that fails with `ENOTFOUND`/`EAI_AGAIN` throws the new exported `KubernetesAgentAddressUnresolvableError` naming the FQDN and pointing at `agentAddress: 'pod-ip'`, and stops retrying immediately instead of spending a connect budget that outlasts the privilege probe's deadline; `VsockTransportOptions` gains the two optional hooks that make that possible, unset everywhere else: the `permanentDialFailure` predicate, and `onDialAttempt`, which fires once per connect attempt before it is made — `exec` and `listFiles` need it because the shared execution controller's 2 s bound on a control request aborts a black-holed connect before the dial's own 5 s timer has failed it, so those two classify by watching their dials rather than by reading an error that no longer describes one. A `'pod-ip'` transport that follows a replaced pod now reports the new uid back to its workspace, so the next `suspend()` retires the pod the session was actually on. The bind token, the privilege probe, egress verification and the guest wire protocol are unchanged. `'pod-ip'` additionally needs a pod network routable from the host and a `NetworkPolicy` admitting its address range — not live-validated, because this machine's kind bed has no route to pod IPs, which is the condition the mode exists for; the in-cluster e2e runner keeps using `'service'`. `.changeset/kubernetes-agent-address.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sandbox` — an optional field and an exported error class, both additive.
- **Update** List past and running orchestration runs with `/agents runs` (`packages/cli/src/tui/slashCommands.ts`, `App.tsx`; new `packages/cli/src/integrations/subagents/runs.ts`; `AgentExplorer.tsx`'s existing cockpit is reused unchanged). `/agents` gains a third subcommand beside `running`/`available`: a table mirroring `/jobs`' own shape — run name, started, phases joined, agents done/total, tokens total, elapsed — one row per parent turn that delegated at least one child, newest first. A run still in flight is read from the live monitor (`liveOrchestrationRuns`, grouping by `workflowId` and excluding a group every one of whose members has already settled — that one belongs to disk instead); a finished run is read cheaply from disk (`listSavedOrchestrationRuns`, one `run.json` read per child through the existing `listChildren`, no transcript) and named from the PARENT turn's own opening words, read via `readRunMessagesIn` on `<sessionId>/runs/<parentRunId>/messages.json` — not a `workflow` label, which [delegation events](sdk/delegation-events.md) already states never survives to disk. A live row is named from its `workflow` label instead, the one case that label is still trustworthy, since the process that would know it is the one being asked. `combineOrchestrationRuns` (`slashCommands.ts`) merges the two lists, the live row winning any id both name, sorted newest first and capped at 20 with an omitted count — the same bound `DelegationHistory`'s own archive listing uses; `renderAgentRuns` states an empty history in words rather than opening an empty picker, and an unrecognised subcommand still shows usage. The disk walk runs behind a disabled `Loading…` picker row rather than blocking the composer: `AgentSession` gains an optional `listOrchestrationRuns()` beside `savedChildren()`. Enter opens the selected run directly into its first agent's transcript in the existing cockpit, so a finished run's `Replayed from saved evidence.` banner is the first thing on screen. [Slash commands](cli/slash-commands.md) and [delegated work](cli/delegated-work.md) gain the discovery path. `.changeset/agents-runs-subcommand.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/cli` — a new operator capability, no existing behavior changed.
- **Update** Open a finished delegated child from the evidence it already writes to disk. New on `@namzu/sdk`: the static `RunDiskStore.listChildren(baseDir, parentRunId)` and the `DelegatedChildRun` type it returns (`store/run/disk.ts`, `types/run/store.ts`). It walks `<baseDir>/<parentRunId>/children/`, reads each `run.json` and reports the run id, its directory and whatever the file recorded of `agentId`, `agentName`, `metadata.config.model`, `status`, `startedAt`, `endedAt`, `tokenUsage.totalTokens` and `depth` — every field optional, because `run.json` is written on a terminal path and a child killed before it got there leaves a transcript worth reading and no ending. Read-only (a bound `RunDiskStore` CREATES its run directory, so discovery is a static walk), tolerant (a directory with no `run.json`, or unreadable JSON, is skipped rather than raised), and ordered oldest-first by `startedAt`. `addToIndex`'s `if (run.parentRunId) return` guard is untouched and `listRuns` is unchanged: this is an additional read, not a fix to the catalogue, and a delegated child still never appears in a conversation listing. [delegation events](sdk/delegation-events.md) gains a section naming the on-disk layout, the three properties and what the child's own log does and does not carry. On the CLI, `integrations/subagents/replay.ts` composes that read across session directories — a child gets its OWN session directory, so one parent run's children are spread across as many as it launched, and the conversation's own `runs/` directory is what names the parent run ids after a restart — and feeds each child's persisted events through `SubagentActivityMonitor`'s existing `begin()`/`onEvent` path in a new replay mode. One projection, two sources: the test that matters asserts a replayed `SubagentActivity` equals, field for field, what the live path produced from the same events. What `run.json` holds and the child's transcript cannot — its delegation outcome and final totals, both reported on the PARENT's stream — is written after the events. `SubagentActivity` gains `replayed`; `AgentTranscript` heads a replayed child with `Replayed from saved evidence. This child cannot be continued.` (paid for out of the page, not by growing the box), `agentMetaParts` marks the row `saved` at every width, and `activeSubagentCohorts` excludes replayed rows so saved work never reads as live. `AgentSession` gains an optional `savedChildren()`; `App` hydrates it when a session is installed, again whenever `/resume`, `/new` or a fork changes which conversation the scope names (which is what makes a previous process's child openable — the children worth opening belong to the conversation just switched TO), and again on every cockpit open (which is what recovers one the 80-agent bound evicted), merging saved rows behind the live ones and dropping any whose run id the live monitor still holds. Opening the cockpit with nothing on screen AWAITS that read before answering, so the first `/agents` after a resume opens the saved children instead of printing `No delegated agents in this conversation.` about a conversation whose evidence is on disk; rows already on screen open immediately. Bounded: at most 2,000 session directories scanned, at most the 80 most recent children replayed. A torn `transcript.jsonl` is read strictly first — the only way to LEARN it is damaged, since the tolerant reader's job is to skip past the damage — then tolerantly, and the replay closes with a row saying the record is partial; a transcript that cannot be read at all opens with that row alone beside what `run.json` recorded, rather than dropping the child from the list. Nothing is created, moved or pruned by opening a past run. Two limits stated rather than papered over: streaming deltas never enter a run's log, so a replayed transcript carries tool calls and failures but not the assistant prose between them (`report.md` has the answer), and delegation events enter no log either, so a replayed child's `workflow`/`phase` labels are not recovered — saved children are grouped by parent run and carry the unlabelled default workflow, exactly as a live child launched without labels does. [delegated-work](cli/delegated-work.md)'s evidence section is rewritten to separate the receipt the model reads from the evidence the operator can open, to say that neither restarts anything, and to state the retention exposure plainly: child directories accumulate, nothing prunes them, and a prune command is follow-up work. `.changeset/replay-child-evidence.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sdk` (additive) and **minor** for `@namzu/cli` (a new operator capability).
- **Update** Carry the delegation display labels on the `agent_pending` event. `RunEvent`'s `agent_pending` variant (`packages/sdk/src/types/run/events.ts`) and the two options types a host delegates through — `CreateTaskOptions` (`types/agent/scheduler.ts`) and `SendMessageOptions` (`types/agent/task.ts`) — gain optional `workflow`, `phase`, `phaseDetail` and `phaseOrder`, threaded through `LocalTaskScheduler.createTask` and both `agent_pending` emission sites in `manager/agent/lifecycle.ts`. The field doc comments repeat the `Agent` tool schema's guarantee in its own words: these are display annotations only; they do not create dependencies, barriers, or serial execution — `planId`/`planStepId` remain the delegation fields carrying correlation a host may act on. Every field is optional and absent unless supplied, so a host that groups nothing emits exactly what it emitted before. The SSE mapper's `agent.pending` allowlist (`bridge/sse/mapper.ts`) carries them as `workflow`, `phase`, `phase_detail`, `phase_order`, each spread conditionally and `phase_order: 0` preserved as the first phase rather than dropped as falsy; the A2A mapper keeps its `null` entry with an extended comment saying the omission is deliberate. The point of moving them is reach, not durability: a listener or SSE consumer outside the delegating process can rebuild the grouping instead of seeing a flat list of children. Delegation events still go straight to a host's listener and enter no run's log, so nothing here survives a restart unless a host records it — [delegation events](sdk/delegation-events.md) says so in its own section, against the `seq` contract that already documented it. On the CLI side the `Agent` tool (`integrations/subagents/runtime.ts`) sends the labels it already collected down onto the delegation, and `SubagentActivityMonitor` (`integrations/subagents/activity.ts`) reads them off the event through the same grouping path `begin()` uses, with the launch-time values kept as the seed and merged under the event's — so a child that fails before `agent_pending` still groups where it was launched, a partially labelled event erases nothing, and for a run supplying the same labels on both paths the grouping is byte-identical to before. Phase identity stays the monitor-owned `phaseId`, never a label. [delegated-work](cli/delegated-work.md)'s plan-correlation paragraph is rewritten: both kinds of annotation now travel on the same event, so the contrast is what they mean, not where they live. `.changeset/delegation-display-labels.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sdk` (additive, all optional) and **patch** for `@namzu/cli` (behaviour unchanged; it now sources the labels from the event).
- **Creation** Add [delegation events](sdk/delegation-events.md), the page describing what a run says on its own listener when it delegates. Names the trigger points as they are: `agent_pending` fires when the child has been created and QUEUED — before admission, so a consumer that renders it as started is wrong, and the CLI shows `Queued` until the child's own `run_started` — then exactly one of `agent_completed`, `agent_failed` or `agent_canceled` when it settles, with `subsession_spawned` arriving on the same listener at admission. Tabulates what `agent_pending` carries and separates the two kinds: `planId`/`planStepId` name an approved plan edge and are correlation a host may act on, while `workflow`, `phase`, `phaseDetail` and `phaseOrder` are captions — display annotations only; they do not create dependencies, barriers, or serial execution. States how each bridge treats them (SSE carries the labels through its allowlist, each key present only when supplied; A2A maps every delegation event to `null`) and, in its own section, that delegation events are not persisted: the agent manager hands them straight to a host's listener without passing through the run's event translator, which is what their absent `seq` means, so a label here buys reach and not durability and a host that wants the grouping to outlive its process records it from the listener.
- **Update** Move the permission-mode badge and the model/effort identity onto [one composer footer line](cli/terminal-design.md#the-composer-footer) (`packages/cli/src/tui/StatusBar.tsx`, `Composer.tsx`, `App.tsx`). The mode badge used to draw as its own row inside the message frame, above the input, only when the mode differed from `prompt` (growing the frame from three rows to four); the model, effort and working directory sat on a separate status line one blank row below the frame. Both now share a single, always-present line directly below the frame, which is now a constant three rows: the mode badge (colored by mode, with its `(shift+tab to cycle)` reminder) or, in `prompt` mode, a quiet `shift+tab to cycle`; the reasoning effort beside it as `· effort <level>`, only when the operator has set one — the previous line's unconditional `<model> default` is gone; the working directory; and, right-aligned, the interaction hint or durable goal exactly as before, or the model identity when neither is active (it moved here from the left). The footer stays exactly one row at every width: the working directory shrinks and drops first, then the effort label, then Shift+Tab's cycle reminder, then the model, and only as a last resort does the mode badge itself truncate. `StatusBar` gains `permissionMode` and `canCycleMode`; `Composer` loses the now-unused `permissionMode` prop (it kept `onCycleMode` for the Shift+Tab key). No mode's behavior, name, icon or color changed — only where and how the mode is drawn. The composer frame plus footer is now one row shorter in the common case, so `AgentExplorer.tsx`'s full-screen cockpit box — sized as `terminalRows` minus a fixed chrome reservation — now uses the named `COCKPIT_CHROME_ROWS` (2, was an inline `3`) so it claims the freed row instead of leaving it unclaimed for a stale transcript line to bleed into. `.changeset/mode-footer.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/cli` — a visible layout change to the interactive TUI, though neither component sits on the package's public entry point.

- **Update** Extend [MCP protocol eras](sdk/mcp-protocol-eras.md#mirroring-tool-parameters-into-headers-x-mcp-header) with `x-mcp-header`: the tool-definition constraints namzu enforces, the `Mcp-Param-*` headers a modern call carries, and the `-32020` recovery (`packages/sdk/src/connector/mcp/x-mcp-header.ts`, `envelope.ts`, `client.ts`). A server may annotate a tool parameter to have its value mirrored into an `Mcp-Param-{name}` request header so an intermediary can route a call without parsing JSON-RPC, and a client on the Streamable HTTP transport must refuse a tool definition whose annotation breaks any of six constraints — non-empty, RFC 9110 `1*tchar`, no CR/LF, case-insensitively unique across the `inputSchema`, applied only to a `string`, `boolean` or `integer` parameter (`number` excluded), and statically reachable from the schema root through `properties` keys alone. `MCPClient.listTools()` now excludes such a tool and logs the name and the reason at `warn`, which is the first place namzu declines to expose something a server offered: the page says so plainly, and says that only annotated tools on that transport can be affected. The page also documents where the validator's subtlety is (it walks the raw schema and never follows a `$ref`, since resolving references first makes a forbidden path look like a permitted one), which connections validate versus mirror (validation follows the transport, as the spec conditions it; the headers follow the era), how a value is written — string as-is, boolean lowercase, integer decimal, then the same base64 sentinel as `Mcp-Name` — and when no header is written at all (an absent or `null` argument, a value whose runtime type contradicts the schema, an integer outside ±(2^53−1)). New exports `validateMcpHeaderAnnotations`, `McpParamHeaderBinding` and `McpHeaderAnnotationVerdict`, plus `McpEnvelopeInput.paramHeaders`. `.changeset/mcp-x-header-validation.md`, **major** for `@namzu/sdk` — a tool a host used to see can now be absent from a listing.

- **Update** Extend [MCP protocol eras](sdk/mcp-protocol-eras.md) with MRTR's `resultType` envelope. New `decodeResult` (`packages/sdk/src/connector/mcp/envelope.ts`) reads any JSON-RPC result past `resultType`: absent or `"complete"` is an ordinary result, unchanged; `"input_required"` becomes `{ inputRequests?, requestState? }`; any other value throws the new `MCPInvalidResultTypeError`, per the spec's own "MUST be considered invalid" — never silently accepted. `MCPClient.callTool` (`client.ts`) retries a `requestState`-only `InputRequiredResult` exactly once, echoing `requestState` byte-for-byte as a top-level `requestState` parameter under a fresh JSON-RPC id (the same convention `listAllPages` already uses for a pagination `cursor`); `requestState` itself is never inspected, parsed or logged in full. An `InputRequiredResult` carrying `inputRequests` this client cannot satisfy, or a second `input_required` after that one retry, throws the new `MCPInputRequiredError` rather than looping. namzu declares `clientCapabilities: {}` in every era, so per MRTR rule 7 a conforming server can only ever produce the `requestState`-only case — the genuinely likely failure for a no-capability host is the separate `-32021 MissingRequiredClientCapability` error. `mcpToolToToolDefinition`'s `execute` (`adapter.ts`) catches both `MCPInputRequiredError` and `-32021` (via the existing `isMissingRequiredClientCapabilityError`) and returns a typed, catchable `ToolResult` instead of an unexplained rejection — `data.code` of `mcp_tool_input_required` (naming the requested method(s)) or `mcp_tool_missing_client_capability` (naming `data.requiredCapabilities`), both `retrySafety: 'safe'` since neither outcome implies the call's side effect ran, and both still pass through `frameServerResult`'s untrusted-content envelope. New on the public surface: `decodeResult`, `MCPDecodedResult`, `MCPInputRequest`, `MCPInputRequiredError`, `MCPInvalidResultTypeError`. Nothing existing changes shape — a legacy result with no `resultType` decodes exactly as before. `.changeset/mcp-result-type-mrtr.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sdk`.

- **Update** Extend [MCP protocol eras](sdk/mcp-protocol-eras.md) with legacy session and stream fidelity — three behaviors the 2025-03-26 through 2025-11-25 Streamable HTTP transports specify that `StreamableHttpTransport` (`packages/sdk/src/connector/mcp/streamable-http.ts`) did not implement, all gated to legacy structurally rather than by an era flag (only a legacy connection ever sends `initialize` and so ever captures a `Mcp-Session-Id` at all). On a `404` from a request carrying a session id, `MCPClient` drops the transport's session (new `StreamableHttpTransport.resetSession()`/`hasSession()`) and re-runs the `initialize`/`notifications/initialized` handshake exactly once (new private `performLegacyInitializeHandshake`, shared with `connect()`'s own first handshake, and `reinitializeLegacySession`), then retries the original request once through a new `requestWithSessionRecovery` wrapper now used by `listTools`/`callTool`/`readResource`/`getPrompt`/`listResourceTemplates`/`listResources`; a second `404` after that surfaces as a failure rather than looping, and `connect()`'s own first `initialize` — which has no session yet to lose — is never itself subject to recovery. `close()` now sends a best-effort, fire-and-forget `DELETE` carrying `Mcp-Session-Id` whenever a session exists, bounded by its own 5s timeout and never awaited, so an unresponsive or already-gone peer cannot delay or fail the transport's existing bounded-teardown guarantee; a modern connection, which never holds a session, sends none. `parseSseMessages` is now exported from `streamable-http.ts` for direct testing (returning a new `MCPSseParseResult` of `{ messages, lastEventId? }` in place of a bare array) — not a public-surface change, since that module has no subpath in the package's `exports` map — and captures the newest SSE `id:` field across a response body — including the empty-data priming event the 2025-11-25 transport mandates — which `StreamableHttpTransport` remembers across a `close()`/`connect()` cycle and sends as `Last-Event-ID` on the next request built on a session; a `:`-prefixed comment/keep-alive line was, and remains, ignored rather than treated as malformed, since both the `data:` and new `id:` extraction already select a line by its own prefix. This client's Streamable HTTP transport buffers each response fully rather than holding a live stream open, so this resumption is a best-effort hint on the next ordinary request rather than the spec's dedicated GET-stream reconnection — narrower than full fidelity, and documented as such. `.changeset/mcp-legacy-session-fidelity.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sdk` — additive behavior on legacy connections only; the zero-config request shape and every modern-era behavior are unchanged.

- **Update** Extend [MCP protocol eras](sdk/mcp-protocol-eras.md): `MCPClient.connect()` (`packages/sdk/src/connector/mcp/client.ts`) now resolves the protocol era by probing for MCP `2026-07-28` before it offers the legacy `initialize` handshake, so the first request a server sees is `server/discover`. Two probes, not one algorithm: over Streamable HTTP a `400`/`404`/`405` is read for a recognized modern JSON-RPC error in its **body** (`-32022`, `-32021`, `-32020`, or `-32601` on a `404` — the case a status alone gets wrong), and over stdio the fallback predicate is `isRecognizedModernError` rather than any specific code, with silence resolving legacy after the new `MCPClientConfig.eraProbeTimeoutMs` (default `2000`, clamped to `requestTimeoutMs`). A `2xx` or a reply that is not a well-formed `DiscoverResult` resolves legacy rather than being taken as proof. A `-32022` intersects `data.supported` with `MCP_SUPPORTED_PROTOCOL_VERSIONS`, takes the newest, and retries at most once; a disjoint intersection refuses, naming both lists. New `connector/mcp/era.ts` holds the state machine plus the per-origin (HTTP) / per-`cwd`-and-command (stdio) `MCPEraCache` — whose key folds the `streamable_http` and `streamable-http` spellings together and keeps `http-sse`, which is never probed, apart — injectable through the new `MCPClientConfig.eraCache`, defaulting to a process-wide instance; a remembered legacy peer skips the probe, a remembered modern one that starts answering as legacy is corrected in that same connect, and a remembered era whose handshake fails is dropped. New `connector/mcp/envelope.ts` holds `buildEnvelope`, which writes a modern request's `_meta` (`io.modelcontextprotocol/protocolVersion` and `clientCapabilities` required, `clientInfo` a SHOULD) and its mirrored `MCP-Protocol-Version` / `Mcp-Method` / `Mcp-Name` headers from one variable, so a mismatched pair is unconstructible rather than validated, plus `encodeMcpHeaderValue` for the `=?base64?…?=` sentinel. That sentinel test is the spec's own — starts with `=?base64?`, ends with `?=`, nothing said about the interior — so a plain value such as `=?base64?a?b?=` is wrapped rather than sent verbatim for a server to fail decoding. The three mirrored headers are the protocol's and not the caller's: a `MCPRequestOptions.headers` entry colliding with `MCP-Protocol-Version`, `Mcp-Method` or `Mcp-Name` (matched case-insensitively) is refused and warn-logged in both eras, so the mismatched pair `buildEnvelope` makes unconstructible cannot be rebuilt one layer up. A modern connection sends no `initialize`, no `notifications/initialized`, no `Mcp-Session-Id`, no `GET`, no `DELETE`, no `Last-Event-ID`, and no `notifications/cancelled` POST over Streamable HTTP (closing the SSE response stream is the cancellation signal there) — stdio still sends it, in every era. `connect()` returns an `MCPInitializeResult` of the same shape in both eras, synthesised from the `DiscoverResult` on a modern origin. A server that answers the legacy `initialize` handshake with a modern revision is now refused, naming the contradiction, rather than recorded as a legacy era at a version that is not a legacy one. `StreamableHttpTransport` now throws `MCPHttpStatusError` carrying the status and the response body it used to discard, with its message text unchanged. New on the public surface: `MCPEraCache`, `MCPDiscoverResult`, `McpEnvelope`, `McpEnvelopeInput`, `McpEraProbe`, `McpEraProbeAnswer`, `McpEraResolution`, `MCPHttpStatusError`, `resolveMcpEra`, `createMcpEraCache`, `defaultMcpEraCache`, `mcpEraCacheKey`, `isRecognizedModernError`, `classifyModernHttpFailure`, `buildEnvelope`, `encodeMcpHeaderValue`, and the `MCP_DISCOVER_METHOD` / `DEFAULT_MCP_ERA_PROBE_TIMEOUT_MS` / `_meta` key / header name constants

- **Creation** Add [MCP protocol eras](sdk/mcp-protocol-eras.md): `MCPClient.connect()` (`packages/sdk/src/connector/mcp/client.ts`) now negotiates across four legacy MCP protocol revisions instead of only `2024-11-05`. `constants/mcp/index.ts` replaces the two single-value `MCP_PROTOCOL_VERSION`/`MCP_SUPPORTED_PROTOCOL_VERSIONS` constants with `MCP_MODERN_VERSIONS` (`['2026-07-28']`, declared but not yet negotiated), `MCP_LEGACY_VERSIONS` (`['2025-11-25', '2025-06-18', '2025-03-26', '2024-11-05']`, newest first) and a derived `MCP_SUPPORTED_PROTOCOL_VERSIONS` union — none of the three are re-exported from the package's public entry, so this is a behavioral default change rather than a removed export. `connect()` offers `MCP_LEGACY_VERSIONS[0]` in exactly ONE `initialize` request (never a per-version waterfall — the spec's own backward-compatibility algorithm offers one version and honors whatever the server answers with) and accepts whatever the server answers with, provided it is in the supported set; a version outside the set is refused, naming the offered version, the answered version, and the full supported list. A server that omits `protocolVersion` is still tolerated, treated as having accepted the offer. New on the public surface: the `McpEra` type (`types/connector/mcp.ts`, `{kind:'modern', version}` or `{kind:'legacy', version}` — only the `legacy` arm is reachable today) and `MCPClient.getEra()`, which returns the era the last `connect()` negotiated. `MCPTransportSendOptions` gains an optional `headers` field so the client — which alone knows the negotiated era — can ask a transport to attach `MCP-Protocol-Version` to one send without the transport needing to know what a protocol version is; `StreamableHttpTransport` merges it over its static config headers. The header is sent on every request following `initialize` when the negotiated version is 2025-06-18 or later (the header did not exist before that revision) and never on `initialize` itself. The docs page also notes that `packages/sdk/src/connector/mcp/server/server.ts` still answers `2024-11-05`, so a namzu-built client now negotiates DOWN against a namzu-built server — expected, not a bug, and out of this workstream's scope. `.changeset/mcp-legacy-era-negotiation.md`, **major** for `@namzu/sdk` — the version `connect()` advertises in `initialize` moves from `2024-11-05` to `2025-11-25`, changing what a server that branches on the client's claimed version sees; there is no per-call override, so a caller needing the old behavior pins the previous major. **minor** for `@namzu/cli` — its MCP servers now negotiate against a broader legacy set through the same client, which is an operator-visible behavior improvement (more servers connect) rather than a change to any CLI-owned public surface or config default.
- **Update** [MCP protocol eras](sdk/mcp-protocol-eras.md) gains a "Per-request authority" section. Both HTTP transports (`packages/sdk/src/connector/mcp/streamable-http.ts`, `http-sse.ts`) took the ambient global `fetch` and a static per-server header map as givens; both now accept an optional `fetch?: MCPFetchLike` transport config field, captured once at construction (`config.fetch ?? fetch`) and used for every HTTP call the transport makes — for `HttpSseTransport` that means both the SSE `GET` and the message `POST`, closing a gap where only `POST` would otherwise have been exercised. `MCPFetchLike` (`types/connector/mcp.ts`) is a new exported type, structurally the same injectable-function idea as `FetchLike` in `bridge/a2a/client.ts` but re-declared rather than imported: both MCP transports read `.headers` and one reads a streamed `.body`, neither of which the A2A bridge's narrower return type exposes, so its return type stays the real `Response` and the A2A bridge is not made to grow fields it does not use. `MCPRequestOptions` gains optional `headers?: Record<string, string>` and `bearerToken?: string`, threaded through `MCPClient.request()` (not `notify()` or the internal cancellation send, which are not caller-shaped requests) into `transport.send()`'s existing `MCPTransportSendOptions.headers`: per-request headers merge over the transport's static config headers, and `bearerToken` is applied last as `Authorization: Bearer …`, so it overrides a configured `Authorization` header without disturbing a differently-named one such as a static `X-API-Key`. `refuseMcpHttpRedirect` is unchanged, so a per-request credential is refused at the same redirect boundary as a static one — proven in the same `http-redirect-boundary.test.ts` assertion rather than a parallel test. Supplying none of the new fields reproduces today's exact request. Also fixed in passing: `HttpSseTransport`'s message `POST` built its headers inline and never read `MCPTransportSendOptions.headers` at all, so a per-send header (including the `MCP-Protocol-Version` header W2 added) silently never reached the wire on that transport; it now has a `buildHeaders()` merge matching `StreamableHttpTransport`'s. `.changeset/mcp-per-request-authority.md`, **minor** for `@namzu/sdk` — every new field is optional and `MCPFetchLike` is additive; the zero-option call surface is unchanged.

- **Update** `MCPClient` (`packages/sdk/src/connector/mcp/client.ts`) now preserves the numeric `code` and untouched `data` payload of a JSON-RPC error reply instead of discarding both into a formatted string.
